头颈癌的空间分析确定了两个具有不同上皮间质转化模式的生态系统 - Nature

Spatial analysis of head and neck cancer identifies two ecosystems with distinct modes of epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition - Nature
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 14 日发表的《自然》研究使用 26 个头颈鳞状细胞癌 (HNSCC) 样本的空间转录组学来绘制 HPV 阳性和 HPV 阴性肿瘤的肿瘤结构图。研究人员发现了部分上皮间质转化(p-EMT)的两种不同的空间模式:一种“边缘”模式,与侵入前沿通过 TGFβ 与成纤维细胞相互作用相关;另一种“核心”模式,由免疫抑制性巨噬细胞和中性粒细胞通过肿瘤巢内的制瘤素 M 驱动。这些模式在同一肿瘤的多个样本中是一致的,表明肿瘤范围的特征对侵袭、免疫逃避和靶向治疗具有潜在的生物学和临床意义。所有数据集和分析代码均可公开访问,但研究结果仍处于初步阶段,需要进一步验证。

时间
2026 年 8 月 14 日发布;提供的证据中未指定研究期限。
地点与对象
通过空间转录组学分析头颈鳞状细胞癌 (HNSCC) 样本,包括 HPV 阳性和 HPV 阴性肿瘤 (n = 26)。所提供的证据中未报告。
事件
研究报告了 HNSCC 部分上皮间质转化 (p-EMT) 的两种不同空间结构:p-EMT 边缘和 p-EMT 核心,对侵袭、免疫调节和靶向治疗具有影响。该研究由学术研究团队进行,并发表在《自然》杂志上。
规模、影响与风险
这是订阅内容的预览,通过您的机构访问 价格可能需要缴纳结账时计算的地方税 本研究中生成的所有转录组数据集均已存放在国家生物技术信息基因表达综合中心。
应对、状态与不确定性
所有数据集和自定义代码均可不受限制地公开获取(登录代码 GSE268014、GSE279017、S-BIAD3612)。研究结果是初步的,需要在临床环境中进一步验证。没有报告公共卫生反应或风险评估。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Spatial analysis of head and neck cancer identifies two ecosystems with distinct modes of epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition - Nature

News Brief
A Nature study published on 14 August 2026 used spatial transcriptomics on 26 head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (HNSCC) samples to map tumor architecture in HPV-positive and HPV-negative tumors. Researchers identified two distinct spatial patterns of partial epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition (p-EMT): an 'edge' pattern linked to fibroblast interactions via TGFβ at invasive fronts, and a 'core' pattern driven by immunosuppressive macrophages and neutrophils via oncostatin M within tumor nests. These patterns were consistent across multiple samples from the same tumor, suggesting tumor-wide features with potential biological and clinical implications for invasion, immune evasion, and targeted therapies. All datasets and analysis code are publicly accessible, but the findings remain preliminary and require further validation.
Original source text (English)
Head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (HNSCC) shows substantial intra- and inter-tumoral heterogeneity. We mapped tumor architecture across HPV-positive and HPV-negative HNSCC through spatial transcriptomics ( n = 26). HPV-positive tumors display hypercellularity, higher lymphocyte presence, enriched hypoxia and reduced partial epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition (p-EMT) in malignant cells. We observed two distinct spatial architectures of p-EMT: p-EMT edge, where p-EMT is coupled to fibroblasts at the invasive front via TGFβ, and p-EMT core, in which tumor-infiltrating immunosuppressive macrophages and neutrophils induce p-EMT via oncostatin M in the core of tumor nests. These two p-EMT patterns were consistent across multiple samples from the same tumor, suggesting they are tumor-wide features. Together, these findings reveal that distinct interactions in the tumor microenvironment converge on a similar p-EMT cellular phenotype, but in a different spatial pattern that may have potential biological and clinical implications for our understanding of invasion, immune modulation and new targeted therapeutics for HNSCC. Location and population
Head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (HNSCC) samples, including HPV-positive and HPV-negative tumors, analyzed via spatial transcriptomics (n = 26). Not reported in the supplied evidence.

The study was conducted by an academic research team and published in Nature.
Scale, impact and risk

Response, status and uncertainty
All datasets and custom code are publicly available without restriction (accession codes GSE268014, GSE279017, S-BIAD3612). Findings are preliminary and require further validation in clinical settings. No public health response or risk assessment is reported.

一种病毒,两种癌症:喉咙中的 HPV 与子宫颈中的 HPV 有何共同点 - 现代加纳

One Virus, Two Cancers: What HPV in the Throat Has in Common With HPV in the Cervix - Modern Ghana
新闻简报A feature in Modern Ghana explains how high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV) types—especially HPV-16 and HPV-18—drive both cervical and oropharyngeal cancers through the same molecular hijacking of host cell checkpoints (E6/E7 proteins). Oral sex is identified as the specific route that delivers HPV to the throat, with large U.S. studies showing a clear dose-response: people with more than ten lifetime oral-sex partners have over four times the odds of HPV-related throat cancer, and starting oral sex before age 18 or with older partners further raises risk. Globally, HPV is the most common sexually transmitted infection and the leading cause of cervical cancer, while HPV-positive throat cancers have surged in higher-income countries. In Ghana, the article highlights the October 2025 launch of a nationwide HPV vaccination campaign for 2.4 million girls aged 9–14, part of efforts to eliminate cervical cancer as a public health problem.

地点与对象
加纳(针对 9-14 岁女孩的全国 HPV 疫苗接种 运动);美国(口腔 HPV 和口咽癌的行为和流行病学研究);HPV 相关癌症的全球背景。
事件
媒体专题解释了 高危型HPV 类型(尤其是 HPV-16 和 HPV-18)如何通过共享的分子机制(E6/E7 蛋白)导致宫颈癌和口咽癌,并强调口交是 传播 通往喉咙的途径。该文章还重点介绍了加纳 2025 年针对少女的全国 HPV 疫苗接种 运动。
规模、影响与风险
全球大多数 宫颈癌 和很大一部分口咽癌都是由 HPV 引起的;HPV 是全球最常见的性传播感染;在高收入国家,HPV 阳性口咽癌急剧上升;加纳每年记录超过 117,000 例新 宫颈癌 病例和 76,000 例死亡;男性携带致癌性口腔 HPV 的可能性大约是女性的六倍;口交伴侣数量与 HPV 相关口咽癌风险之间的剂量反应关系。
应对、状态与不确定性
Ghana launched a nationwide HPV vaccination campaign in October 2025 targeting 2.4 million girls aged 9–14, aiming to prevent tens of thousands of future cervical cancer deaths and align with WHO’s elimination target; no routine screening test exists for HPV-related oropharyngeal cancer; symptoms (persistent sore throat, difficulty swallowing, neck lump, unresolved ear pain) often appear late; condoms reduce but do not eliminate HPV transmission risk.

Oral sex is identified as the specific route that delivers HPV to the throat, with large U.S. studies showing a clear dose-response: people with more than ten lifetime oral-sex partners have over four times the odds of HPV-related throat cancer, and starting oral sex before age 18 or with older partners further raises risk. Original source text (English)
Home Radio TV Videos Movies Advertise Ghana Contact Login NEWS SPORTS BUSINESS POLITICS OPINIONS HEALTH AFRICA WORLD ENTERTAINMENT LIFESTYLE SCIENCE TECHNOLOGY NOLLYWOOD MEDIA MEMBERS Archive Health News Family/Parenting Home/Food Health/Fitness Spirituality OnAIR Search Tue, 11 Aug 2026 Health & Fitness One Virus, Two Cancers: What HPV in the Throat Has in Common With HPV in the Cervix By Dakudzie Emerald & Gbadegbe Nada Most people have heard that the human papillomavirus (HPV) causes cervical cancer. Far fewer know that the same virus is now one of the leading causes of throat cancer, and that infections in both parts of the body are transmitted, and switch on cancer, in strikingly similar ways. As Ghana rolls out its first nationwide HPV vaccination campaign for adolescent girls, it is worth understanding why this one virus deserves attention well beyond the cervix. A virus with more than 200 faces HPV is not a single virus but a family of more than 200 related viruses that infect skin and mucous membranes. Most types are harmless and clear on their own within one to two years. A smaller group, classified as "high-risk" types, can persist in the body and gradually push infected cells toward cancer. Two of these, HPV-16 and HPV-18, are responsible for the majority of HPV-related cancers worldwide, including most cervical cancers and a large share of oropharyngeal (throat) cancers. HPV spreads through direct skin-to-skin and mucosal contact during vaginal, anal, and oral sex. It does not require the exchange of bodily fluids the way HIV or hepatitis B does, which is part of why it is the most common sexually transmitted infection globally, and why condoms, though protective, do not eliminate risk entirely since they don't cover all exposed skin. Oral sex: the specific route into the throat Because oropharyngeal cancer is the less familiar of the two diseases, it is worth being precise about how HPV actually gets into the throat, since the evidence here comes from some of the largest behavioural studies conducted on the subject. The clearest data come from a landmark U.S. case-control study led by epidemiologist Gypsyamber D'Souza and colleagues, which compared 100 patients with oropharyngeal cancer to 200 matched cancer-free controls. Among the patients with cancer, 88 percent reported having had at least one lifetime oral-sex partner, and oral HPV-16 infection was found in 32 percent of cancer patients versus just 4 percent of controls, while any oral HPV infection was found in 37 percent of patients versus 6 percent of controls. That study was central to establishing oral sex, specifically, rather than sexual activity in general, as a driver of this cancer. Discover more Medical Literature & Resources cancers cancer A larger follow-up study by the same research group, published in 2021 and covering 163 oropharyngeal cancer patients and 345 matched controls, found that people with more than 10 lifetime oral-sex partners had over four times the odds of developing HPV-related oropharyngeal cancer compared with those with fewer partners. The same study found that starting oral sex before age 18, having oral sex with partners at least ten years older, and having a higher "intensity" of oral-sex partners over a short period were each independently linked to higher risk, on top of partner count alone. National survey data collected in the United States (NHANES) similarly show a clear gradient: oral HPV was found in fewer than 2.5 percent of people who reported never having performed oral sex, rising to as much as 14 to 15 percent among men who had both smoked and had five or more lifetime oral-sex partners. Across that same national dataset, men were roughly six times more likely than women to carry oncogenic oral HPV, which lines up with the fact that HPV-related throat cancer affects men several times more often than women worldwide. None of this means oral sex is unusually dangerous compared to other forms of sexual contact, or that people who have had oral sex will develop cancer; the great majority of oral HPV infections, like cervical ones, clear on their own. What the data show is a dose-response relationship: the more lifetime oral-sex exposure to HPV a person has, particularly starting at a younger age or with more partners, the higher their cumulative risk of a persistent infection that could, over decades, progress to cancer. The same molecular hijack, two different addresses What links cervical cancer and HPV-driven throat cancer is not just the virus's name, but its precise mechanism of harm. When a high-risk HPV type infects the basal cells of the cervix or the tissue lining the tonsils and base of the tongue, it produces two small viral proteins, E6 and E7, that hijack the host cell's own safety systems. Ordinarily, a cell has built-in checkpoints that stop damaged or abnormally dividing cells from surviving and multiplying. The E6 protein binds to and marks for destruction a tumour-suppressor protein called p53, often described as the "guardian of the genome," which would otherwise force damaged cells to stop dividing or self-destruct. Around the same time, the E7 protein disables another checkpoint protein, the retinoblastoma protein (Rb), which normally keeps cells from dividing until they are ready. With both brakes released, infected cells are pushed to keep dividing even as genetic damage accumulates, and cells that should have died instead survive, replicate, and can eventually pass this instability on to daughter cells. Over years, this can progress from precancerous changes to invasive cancer. Discover more infection infections HEALTH Because this E6/E7 mechanism is the same whether the infected tissue is cervical epithelium or the lymphoid tissue of the tonsils and tongue base, HPV-driven cervical cancer and HPV-driven oropharyngeal cancer are, at the molecular level, closely related diseases arising in different organs. Where the two cancers diverge Despite the shared mechanism, the two cancers differ in important ways that matter for prevention and detection. Cervical cancer overwhelmingly affects women and has a well-established screening pathway: Pap smears and HPV DNA tests can catch precancerous cervical changes years before they become cancer, which is why organised screening programmes have driven down cervical cancer rates wherever they have been sustained. HPV-related oropharyngeal cancer, by contrast, disproportionately affects men, and there is no equivalent routine screening test for the throat. Research tracking cancer registries has found that HPV-positive throat cancers have risen sharply in higher-income countries over the past two decades, even as tobacco-related throat cancers have declined, so that HPV is now estimated to cause a majority of oropharyngeal cancers in some developed countries. Because there is no early-warning test, these cancers are often diagnosed only after a person notices a persistent sore throat, difficulty swallowing, an unexplained lump in the neck, or ear pain that does not resolve, symptoms easy to dismiss until the disease is more advanced. As the data above show, risk rises specifically with the number and pattern of oral-sex partners, and is compounded further by smoking and a weakened immune system, all of which make it harder for the body to clear a persistent high-risk HPV infection before it can trigger these changes. The burden, in numbers Cervical cancer remains the fourth most common cancer in women globally, with roughly 660,000 new cases and 350,000 deaths a year, around 90 percent of them in low- and middle-income countries. In Ghana specifically, there were an estimated 3,072 new cervical cancer cases in 2023. Across sub-Saharan Africa, the burden is especially heavy: the region records well over 117,000 new cervical cancer cases and more than 76,000 deaths annually, the highest incidence of any region in the world, largely because screening and vaccination coverage have historically lagged behind higher-income countries. Throat cancer's HPV story is a more recent one. Global estimates attribute roughly 30 to 43 percent of oropharyngeal cancers to HPV infection, with some high-income countries reporting figures well above that. In the United States, HPV-positive throat cancer cases rose by more than 200 percent between 1988 and 2004, and HPV is now believed to cause around 70 percent of oropharyngeal cancers in similar developed-country settings. Men are consistently affected several times more often than women. Why this matters for Ghana right now In October 2025, Ghana Health Service, together with Gavi, the WHO, and UNICEF, launched the country's first nationwide HPV vaccination campaign, aiming to reach about 2.4 million girls aged 9 to 14 with a vaccine that is now being folded into the routine immunisation schedule. This is expected to prevent tens of thousands of future cervical cancer deaths, and it lines up Ghana with the WHO's global target of eliminating cervical cancer as a public health problem by pushing incidence below 4 cases per 100,000 women. What is less discussed locally is that the same vaccine also protects against the HPV types responsible for most throat cancers, and current guidance supports vaccinating boys as well as girls, since men bear a disproportionate share of the HPV-related throat cancer burden and unvaccinated men also continue to transmit the virus. A vaccination conversation framed only around "protecting girls from cervical cancer" leaves out half the picture, and half the population that could benefit. What actually works The core prevention message is genuinely simple, even if the biology behind it is complex: Get the HPV vaccine, ideally before the start of sexual activity, since it works best before a person is exposed to the virus. In Ghana, this is now free for eligible girls through the national programme. Attend cervical screening on the recommended schedule if you are a woman old enough to be eligible; it remains the only proven way to catch cervical changes before they become cancer. Practise safer sex, including barrier protection during oral sex and being mindful that risk rises with the number of oral-sex partners and with starting oral sex at a younger age, recognising that barrier methods reduce but do not eliminate risk. Avoid smoking, which independently increases the risk of HPV-related cancers and appears to make it harder for the body to clear persistent infection. Take unexplained, persistent throat or neck symptoms seriously and seek medical evaluation, since there is no routine screening test to catch HPV-related throat cancer early the way a Pap smear can for cervical cancer. A shared virus deserves a shared conversation Cervical cancer and HPV-related throat cancer are often discussed as though they belong to entirely separate conversations, one about women's reproductive health, the other barely discussed at all. But they are driven by the same virus, using the same molecular playbook, and they respond to the same core prevention tools: vaccination, informed sexual health choices, and not smoking. As Ghana pushes forward with its HPV vaccination campaign, broadening the public conversation to include throat cancer, and to include boys and men, could close a gap in awareness before it becomes a gap in survival. Original title (Korean)
"It's not another disease, it's a vaccine that prevents cancer." It's frustrating that the vaccinati.. - 매일경제
News Brief
The article, published on August 11, 2026, highlights the low HPV vaccination rate among male adolescents in South Korea, with only 19.4% of boys born in 2014 having received the first dose as of the 26th of last month. The National Immunization Program (NIP) expanded HPV vaccination to male adolescents this year, but the rate remains significantly lower than that for female adolescents, which exceeds 90%. Director Ha Jung-hoon, a pediatric expert, emphasizes the importance of HPV vaccination in preventing cancer-related diseases and reducing medical costs. He also suggests combining HPV vaccination with other vaccines during adolescence to improve uptake. The article notes that the number of male HPV reports increased by 83% from 2020 to 2024, and male patients in Korea were 45,586 as of last year, about five times that of female patients. Despite the expansion of the program, the vaccination rate for male adolescents is still insufficient, and public health efforts are needed to address this gap.

National vaccination (NIP) for human papillomavirus (HPV), which had been only applied to women, has been expanded to male adolescents since this year. Three months have passed since the scope of support has been expanded to 12-year-old boys, but the vaccination rate is insignificant. According to the Korea Centers for Disease Control and Prevention on the 11th, as of the 26th of last month, the first HPV vaccination rate of boys born in 2014 was only 19.4%. Ha Jung-hoon, the author of Korea's representative parenting guide "Pipopppop119 Pediatrics," and Ha Jung-hoon, the director of Pediatrics, who has been in the clinical field for more than 30 years, is a mentor to Korean parents. Director Ha responded to the Mail Business interview, saying, "It is regrettable that we are experiencing the best period of vaccination." Director Ha said, "Because immune responses are active in adolescence, the more you get vaccinated at this time, the higher the prevention effect and longer it lasts," adding, "The currently approved tetravalent vaccine can prevent about 70% of cervical cancer and up to 97% of 9-valent vaccines." The problem is that there are still many people who misunderstand the HPV vaccine as a 'cervical cancer vaccine' and do not vaccinate it. In the early stages of its introduction, the emphasis on women-centered disease prevention data widened the gap in inoculation rates between men and women. Director Ha said, "The HPV vaccination rate for girls exceeds 90%, but the number of boys has been in single digits for several years. In most developed countries, the male and female vaccination rate remains equal." Prevention is more important as men are also not free from the risk of HPV infection and the incidence of related diseases is increasing. The number of male HPV reports increased by 83% from 117 in 2020 to 214 in 2024, according to the KDCA. In particular, the number of male patients in Korea was 45,586 as of last year, about five times that of women. "Since the NIP registration, the number of male adolescents has gradually increased, but it is not yet sufficient," Director Ha said. "All vaccinations are important, but the HPV vaccine is especially meaningful in that it can reduce the enormous burden of medical expenses related to cancer." In order to increase the vaccination rate of male adolescents, it is necessary to actively utilize simultaneous vaccination with other vaccines. Director Ha said, "In adolescence, parents don't visit hospitals often, unlike in infants and toddlers, so it is efficient to take the vaccine they need when they visit the hospital," adding, "If a boy's NIP age group is lowered by one year, it can be combined with simultaneous vaccination with tetanus, diphtheria, and Baekilhae (Tdap) vaccines, which are mainly for 11-year-olds." Adolescents and adults over the age of 13 who are not eligible for NIP support must also be vaccinated to catch up. Director Ha said, "The key to HPV vaccines is to get them before infection, and even after the NIP age, if they are before exposure to the virus, the vaccination value is high," adding, "Women need to get them before the age of 45 and men need to get them before the age of 26." He also expressed the opinion that the government should convert the NIP-supported vaccine from 4 to 9 in the future. The 9-valent vaccine can prevent five more high-risk viruses compared to tetravalent. Ha said, "Most Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) countries, as well as countries that are less financially comfortable than us, support the 9-valent vaccine. There is no reason not to be medically hit by 9," he said. From a public health perspective, male vaccination is also essential. If only women are vaccinated, an inoculation rate of 80-90% is required to form herd immunity, but if men and women are vaccinated together, it can be 60-70%. "The HPV vaccine has been vaccinated more than 500 million worldwide and is sufficiently proven to be introduced by 37 out of 38 OECD countries," Ha said. "The safety of the vaccine should be considered together with scientific statistics and risks when not inoculated, not individual papers, cases, or rumors." <Terminology> Human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine Vaccines that block anal cancer, head and neck cancer, and genital warts (difficulty), which occur in both men and women, including cervical cancer in women. The article was published on 2026-08-11. The vaccination rate for boys born in 2014 was reported as 19.4% as of the 26th of last month.
Location and population
The article discusses the situation in South Korea, focusing on male adolescents and adults, particularly those born in 2014 and aged 12-13 years.
Event
The article reports that the HPV vaccination rate for male adolescents in South Korea is low, with only 19.4% of boys born in 2014 having received the first dose as of the 26th of last month. It also highlights the expansion of the national vaccination program (NIP) to include male adolescents this year.
Scale, impact and risk
The vaccination rate for male adolescents is significantly lower than that for female adolescents, with the latter exceeding 90%. The article notes that the number of male HPV reports increased by 83% from 2020 to 2024, and male patients in Korea were 45,586 as of last year, about five times that of female patients. The HPV vaccine is emphasized as important for reducing medical expenses related to cancer.
Response, status and uncertainty
The vaccination rate for male adolescents remains low, and the National Institute of Public Health and the Korea Centers for Disease Control and Prevention have reported these figures. The article also mentions the need for public health awareness and government policy changes to increase vaccination rates.

宾夕法尼亚大学牙科研究:抗病毒药物 和抗菌口香糖针对与头颈癌相关的 HPV 和口腔病原体 - MedicalResearch.com

UPenn Dental Research: Antiviral and Antibacterial Chewing Gum Targets HPV and Oral Pathogens Linked to Head and Neck Cancer - MedicalResearch.com
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚大学牙科医学院的研究人员开发了一种 抗病毒药物 和抗菌口香糖,旨在减少高危 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 以及口腔病原体牙龈卟啉单胞菌和具核梭杆菌,这些病原体与头颈鳞状细胞癌 (HNSCC) 有关。在离体临床研究中,单个 治疗 聚集了 HNSCC 患者唾液中 93% 的 HPV 和口腔冲洗样本中 80% 的 HPV,而抗菌成分则使唾液和口腔冲洗样本中的牙龈卟啉单胞菌和具核梭菌减少了 99% 以上 (n=42)。研究小组计划进行一项临床试验,评估每天三块牙龈,持续四个星期,以测量 HPV 病毒载量的变化。这种口香糖选择性地针对病原微生物,而不伤害口腔共生体或胃肠道微生物,为预防 HPV 相关的 HNSCC 进展提供了一种潜在的预防方法。

2026 年 8 月 10 日发布;该事件被描述为正在进行的研究和离体临床研究以及计划的临床试验。

宾夕法尼亚大学牙科医学院,费城,宾夕法尼亚州,美国;研究人群包括头颈鳞状细胞癌 (HNSCC) 患者。

宾夕法尼亚大学的研究人员报告了 抗病毒药物 和抗菌口香糖的开发,旨在减少与头颈癌相关的 高危型HPV 和口腔病原体(牙龈卟啉单胞菌、具核梭杆菌);研究结果是初步的,基于离体临床研究和计划的临床试验。

离体研究:单个 治疗 聚集了 HNSCC 患者唾液中 93% 的 HPV 和口腔冲洗样本中 80% 的 HPV;抗菌成分将唾液和口腔冲洗液样本中的牙龈卟啉单胞菌和具核梭杆菌减少了 99% 以上 (n=42)。尚未报告临床疗效或安全性结果;风险被认为是 HPV 相关 HNSCC 进展的潜在预防。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

UPenn Dental Research: Antiviral and Antibacterial Chewing Gum Targets HPV and Oral Pathogens Linked to Head and Neck Cancer - MedicalResearch.com

News Brief
Researchers at the University of Pennsylvania School of Dental Medicine have developed an antiviral and antibacterial chewing gum designed to reduce high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV) and oral pathogens Porphyromonas gingivalis and Fusobacterium nucleatum, which are linked to head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (HNSCC). In ex vivo clinical studies, a single treatment aggregated 93% of HPV in saliva and 80% in oral-rinse samples from HNSCC patients, while the antibacterial component reduced P. gingivalis and F. nucleatum by more than 99% in saliva and oral-rinse samples (n=42). The team plans a clinical trial evaluating three gums per day for four weeks to measure changes in HPV viral load. The gum selectively targets pathogenic microbes without harming oral commensals or gastrointestinal organisms, offering a potential prophylactic approach to prevent HPV-associated HNSCC progression.

VIDEOS UPenn Dental Research: Antiviral and Antibacterial Chewing Gum Targets HPV and Oral Pathogens Linked to Head and Neck Cancer 10 Aug UPenn Dental Research: Antiviral and Antibacterial Chewing Gum Targets HPV and Oral Pathogens Linked to Head and Neck Cancer Posted at 00:59h in Cancer Research, Dental Research, HPV by Marie Benz MD FAAD MedicalResearch.com Interview with: Henry Daniell, PhD Vice-Chair and W.D. Miller Professor Department of Basic & Translational Sciences School of Dental Medicine, University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia PA Dr. Henry Daniell, Ph.D. MedicalResearch.com: What is the background for this study? What bacteria or viruses are associated with head and neck squamous cell carcinomas? Dr. Daniell: Human Papilloma Virus (HPV), anaerobic bacteria Porphyromonas gingivalis (Pg) and Fusobacterium nucleatum (Fn) correlate with worse survival of head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (HNSCC). In tumorigenic cells HPV genome integrates into the host cell genome causing genome instability and genic alteration which facilitate mutation and hyperproliferation. One third of males in the globe is HPV positive and one fifth are infected with high risk HPV16 strain. Pg is invasive and replicates within tumor cells and influences oncogenic signaling of HNSCC. Fn triggers cancer progression by upregulating several oncogenes responsible for inflammation, cell proliferation, invasion, metastasis. Therefore, it is important to control HPV, Pg, and Fn in the oral cavity to reduce initiation or advancement of oral cancer. MedicalResearch.com: What are the main findings of chewing gum with Bean-lectin (FRIL) gum? Dr. Daniell: Antiviral action of FRIL — Flt3 Receptor Interacting Lectin (FRIL) present in lablab bean contains four carbohydrate-binding domains that bind efficiently to complex N-glycans present on virus surface, thereby entrapping virus into large aggregates thereby blocking viral entry into host cells. In addition, FRIL blocks viruses at the endosome level in host cells upon entry. In our Ex vivo clinical study, Bean-lectin (FRIL) gum single treatment (trapped) aggregated 93% of HPV in saliva and 80% in oral-rinse samples from HNSCC patients. Higher aggregation is saliva is due to higher viral load in saliva than oral rinse samples. However, in our clinical trial, we will evaluate three gums per day for four weeks duration to evaluate changes in HPV viral load. MedicalResearch.com: Does the gum affect any other oral or gastrointestinal organisms, i.e. Helicobacter pylori? Dr. Daniell: Our antibacterial chewing gum contains the antimicrobial peptide protegrin. In Ex vivo clinical studies, single dose of bean gum + protegrin-1 reduced Pg/Fn > 99% in saliva and oral-rinse samples (n = 42). However, in our clinical trial, we will evaluate three gums per day for four weeks duration to evaluate changes in HPV viral load. Although protegrin was effective against anaerobic Pg/Fn, it did not kill capsule forming bacteria including Streptococci, providing selectivity and protection of oral commensal bacteria. However, this selectivity is not a property of protegrin, but several oral bacteria protect themselves via capsules or biofilms. It is unlikely that gastrointestinal organisms will be affected because protegrin could be degraded by acids and enzymes in the stomach. MedicalResearch.com: What should readers take away from your report? Dr. Daniell: Very little attention is paid to oral cancer related oral microbiome. Current treatments focus on surgical removal of tumors followed by radiation or chemotherapy. These treatments damage salivary glands, reduce saliva production, cause oral mucositis, and increase abundance of Candida albicans. Therefore, it is important to evaluate antiviral chewing gum at the onset of HNSCC before lesions advance to tumors, antibacterial gum during oral mucositis and antifungal gum post-radiation therapy. For related context on how oral bacteria are linked to HNSCC risk, see this MedicalResearch.com interview on oral bacterial species linked to a 50% increased risk of developing head and neck squamous cell carcinoma. MedicalResearch.com: Is there anything else you would like to add? Any disclosures? Dr. Daniell: In addition to initiation of tumor, Pg, Fn and C. albicans secrete carcinogenic metabolites like acetaldehyde by converting glucose or alcohol. Therefore, elimination of these pathogenic microbes should help oral cancer patients at early stages of onset. While clinical trials are conducted in HNSCC or mucositis patients to evaluate safety and efficacy, antiviral/antibacterial/antifungal chewing gums could be used as prophylaxis to prevent infection by these pathogenic microbes. Disclosures: None disclosed. Citation: Daniell, H., Wakade, G., Singh, R. et al. Ex vivo HNSCC clinical studies using saliva and antiviral or antibacterial chewing gums reveal reduction in carcinogenic microbes. Sci Rep16, 7886 (2026). https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-39062-w Wakade G, Liu SC, Diep NE, Daniell H. 印度验证本土 HPV 检测可实现经济实惠的 宫颈癌 筛查 - usthadian.com

India Validates Indigenous HPV Test for Affordable Cervical Cancer Screening - usthadian.com
新闻简报印度已验证本土 HPV 检测 Truenat HR-HPV-Plus,可进行负担得起的 宫颈癌 筛查,符合 WHO 和 IARC 国际标准。该测试由 Molbio Diagnostics 开发,并由新德里 AIIMS、ICMR 和 IARC France 进行评估,可检测出 8 种 高危型HPV 类型,占 宫颈癌 病例的 95%。研究人员评估了 1,159 个宫颈样本,包括癌前病变和癌症病例,显示出较高的准确性和可重复性。经过验证的测试预计将减少对进口筛查技术的依赖,降低成本,并改善早期检测的机会,特别是在农村和城乡结合部地区。公共卫生专家建议30岁及以上女性进行HPV筛查,以实现早期诊断并减少诊断。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-14;根据 WHO 和 IARC 基准进行的验证研究,所提供的证据中未明确说明事件日期。
地点与对象
印度;经验证适用于 30 岁及以上女性;由 Molbio Diagnostics 开发;由 AIIMS 新德里、ICMR 和 IARC France 进行评估。
事件
用于 宫颈癌 筛查的本土 HPV 检测 (Truenat HR-HPV-Plus) 得到验证,据 AIIMS 新德里、ICMR 和 IARC France 报告,其符合国际性能标准。
规模、影响与风险
该测试检测到八种 高危型HPV 类型,占 宫颈癌 案例的近 95%;使用 1,159 个宫颈样本进行评估,包括癌前病变和癌症病例;有望减少对进口HPV筛查技术的依赖,降低大规模筛查的成本。
应对、状态与不确定性
由 DBT 和 BIRAC 在印度重大挑战下支持的验证研究;测试与现有的 Truenat 诊断基础设施兼容,以便在区和分区医疗机构中部署;所提供的证据中没有报告未解决的信息。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

India Validates Indigenous HPV Test for Affordable Cervical Cancer Screening - usthadian.com

News Brief
India has validated an indigenous HPV test, Truenat HR-HPV-Plus, for affordable cervical cancer screening, meeting WHO and IARC international standards. Developed by Molbio Diagnostics and evaluated by AIIMS New Delhi, ICMR, and IARC France, the test detects eight high-risk HPV types responsible for 95% of cervical cancer cases. Researchers assessed 1,159 cervical samples, including precancerous lesions and cancer cases, demonstrating high accuracy and reproducibility. The validated test is expected to reduce reliance on imported screening technologies, lower costs, and improve access to early detection, particularly in rural and semi-urban areas. Public health experts recommend HPV screening for women aged 30 and above to enable early diagnosis and reduce mortality.

Skip to content August 15, 2026 1:37 am हिन्दी தமிழ் Languages English हिन्दी தமிழ் English हिन्दी தமிழ் Login No products in the cart. ₹0.00 0 Cart Daily Current Affairs International National Economy Science Technology Defence Appointments Sports Environment Daily Current Affairs International National Economy Science Technology Defence Appointments Sports Environment UPSC Current Affairs SSC Current Affairs RRB Current Affairs Banking Current Affairs TNPSC Current Affairs UPSC Current Affairs SSC Current Affairs RRB Current Affairs Banking Current Affairs TNPSC Current Affairs Home - Science Technology India Validates Indigenous HPV Test for Affordable Cervical Cancer Screening Updated - June 11, 2026 10:40 am CURRENT AFFAIRS: Truenat HR-HPV-Plus, AIIMS New Delhi, ICMR, Cervical Cancer Screening, Human Papillomavirus, IARC, Molbio Diagnostics, Women’s Health, WHO Standards, Public Health Full Article One Liner Quiz Mode CA PDF Indigenous HPV Test Receives International Validation India Validates Indigenous HPV Test for Affordable Cervical Cancer Screening: Researchers from AIIMS New Delhi, Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), and the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC), France have successfully validated an indigenous Human Papillomavirus (HPV) test for cervical cancer screening. The validated test, Truenat HR-HPV-Plus, meets international performance standards and is expected to provide an affordable and accessible screening solution for women across India, especially those above 30 years of age. Static GK fact: AIIMS New Delhi was established in 1956 under the All India Institute of Medical Sciences Act, 1956, and functions under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. Why HPV Screening Remains Essential HPV infection is the leading cause of cervical cancer, one of the most common cancers affecting women worldwide. Although HPV vaccination helps prevent future infections, regular screening remains essential for early detection of precancerous changes. Experts recommend HPV screening for women aged 30 years and above, as early diagnosis significantly improves treatment outcomes and reduces mortality. Static GK Tip: Cervical cancer develops in the cervix and is primarily caused by persistent infection with high-risk HPV types. Features of Truenat HR-HPV-Plus Among four indigenous HPV DNA tests evaluated, Truenat HR-HPV-Plus, developed by Molbio Diagnostics, was the only test to satisfy all international validation criteria established by WHO and IARC. The test detects eight high-risk HPV types responsible for nearly 95% of cervical cancer cases. It also demonstrated high accuracy, reliable reproducibility across laboratories, and suitability for use in district and sub-district healthcare facilities. Static GK fact: Molbio Diagnostics is an Indian biotechnology company known for developing the Truenat molecular diagnostic platform. Validation Study and Public Health Significance The validation study was supported by the Department of Biotechnology (DBT) and Biotechnology Industry Research Assistance Council (BIRAC) under the Grand Challenges India initiative. Researchers evaluated four Indian-developed HPV tests using 1,159 cervical samples, including confirmed precancerous lesions and cervical cancer cases. The study followed internationally accepted WHO and IARC validation benchmarks. Static GK Tip: BIRAC is a not-for-profit public sector enterprise under the Department of Biotechnology, promoting innovation in biotechnology and healthcare. Impact on India’s Healthcare System The validated indigenous test is expected to reduce India’s dependence on imported HPV screening technologies and lower the cost of large-scale cervical cancer screening. Its compatibility with the existing Truenat diagnostic infrastructure will enable rapid deployment in rural and semi-urban healthcare centres, improving access to early detection and supporting India’s efforts to reduce cervical cancer burden. Static GK fact: ICMR, established in 1911 and headquartered in New Delhi, is India’s apex body for biomedical research under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Sialadenoma papilliferum of the floor of the mouth with HPV Positivity: a rare case report and literature review - Springer Nature Link

News Brief
A 25-year-old male presented with a rare benign salivary gland tumor, sialadenoma papilliferum (SP), located in the floor of the mouth. Histopathological examination confirmed the diagnosis, while molecular analysis detected HPV-11 DNA via PCR-RFLP, marking the first reported case of HPV-11 positivity in SP. The authors emphasize that this finding is incidental and not associated with malignancy in this context. The report highlights the need for further research to elucidate the biological role of HPV in SP and to refine molecular understanding of this rare lesion. No public health implications or preventive measures were discussed.
Original source text (English)

Sibel Elif Gülteki̇n1 Show authors 27 Accesses Explore all metrics Abstract Sialadenoma papilliferum (SP) is a rare benign salivary gland tumor characterized by a distinctive biphasic architecture and clinical resemblance to other papillary lesions of the oral cavity. Although recent studies have identified alterations in the MAPK pathway, particularly BRAF V600E mutations, the molecular landscape of this rare lesion remains incompletely characterized. We report a case of SP arising in the floor of the mouth of a 25-year-old male patient. Histopathological examination confirmed the diagnosis of SP, while immunohistochemical analysis revealed p63 positivity and negative staining for BRAF V600E and p16. Molecular analysis detected HPV-11 DNA by PCR-RFLP. To the best of the authors’ knowledge, this is the first reported case of HPV-11 DNA detection in SP. Further studies are needed to clarify the biological significance of HPV detection and to better characterize the molecular pathways involved in the development of this rare lesion. 超越 宫颈癌:为什么 HPV 相关的头颈癌需要更多关注 -expresshealthcare.in

Beyond Cervical cancer: Why HPV-related head and neck cancers need greater attention - expresshealthcare.in
新闻简报一位印度资深肿瘤学家警告说,与 HPV 相关的头颈癌在全球和印度都在激增,目前占印度病例的三分之一,但地区差异很大——东方接近一半的患者,而西方则不到五分之一。与烟草相关肿瘤不同,HPV 阳性口咽癌多发于年轻人,对 治疗 的反应要好得多,但许多临床医生仍然忽视妇科疾病之外的 HPV。评论敦促更广泛的 HPV 疫苗接种、在口咽癌检查中进行常规 HPV 检测以及在高负担地区进行有针对性的预防,并指出目前的数据仍然分散,而且筛查这些癌症很困难。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 12 日;该事件被描述为当代公共卫生讨论,没有具体的 暴发 日期。
地点与对象
印度;国家范围与地区差异(印度东部与印度西部);受影响人群包括头颈癌患者和临床医生。
事件
一位资深放射肿瘤学家的评论强调了印度 HPV 相关头颈癌不断增加的负担和独特的 诊断,强调预防、诊断 和临床医生意识方面的差距。

在全球范围内,过去 50 年来,与 HPV 相关的头颈癌增加了 225% 以上;在印度,头颈癌中的 HPV 患病率 合并率为 33%,存在地区差异(印度东部为 47%,印度西部低于 20%);HPV 阳性口咽癌的预后(5 年生存率 >80%)比 HPV 阴性病例(<50%)更好。
应对、状态与不确定性
呼吁将 HPV 检测纳入口咽癌的诊断检查,加强临床医生教育,采用多学科护理途径,并将 HPV 疫苗接种 扩展到 宫颈癌 预防之外;区域负担的特征仍然不充分,研究差距依然存在。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Beyond Cervical cancer: Why HPV-related head and neck cancers need greater attention - expresshealthcare.in

News Brief
A senior Indian oncologist warns that HPV-related head and neck cancers are surging globally and in India, where they now account for a third of cases but with sharp regional differences—reaching nearly half of patients in the east versus under one-fifth in the west. Unlike tobacco-linked tumors, HPV-positive oropharyngeal cancers strike younger adults and respond far better to treatment, yet many clinicians still overlook HPV outside gynecologic disease. The commentary urges wider HPV vaccination, routine HPV testing in oropharyngeal cancer workups, and targeted prevention in high-burden regions, noting that current data remain fragmented and that screening for these cancers is difficult.
Original source text (English)
Express Healthcare Close the sidebar Express Healthcare Beyond Cervical cancer: Why HPV-related head and neck cancers need greater attention Dr Janaki MG, Senior Consultant – Radiation Oncology, Ramaiah Institute of Oncosciences, Ramaiah Memorial Hospital, discusses the evolving role of HPV in head and neck cancers, the gaps in India’s current approach and why prevention, including wider HPV vaccination, needs greater attention By Dr Janaki MG on August 12, 2026 Globally, Human Papilloma Virus (HPV) related head and neck cancers have surged by more than 225 per cent over the past 50 years, even as tobacco- and alcohol-driven cases have declined in many developed nations, according to a GLOBOCAN-based analysisi. In India, a systematic review and meta-analysis of over 4,600 head and neck cancer patients found the overall pooled HPV prevalence to be 33 per cent with striking regional variation, ranging from 47 percent in Eastern India to under 20 percent in Western Indiaii. HPV is responsible for nearly all cervical cancer cases while an emerging and often under-appreciated dimension of HPV-related disease is its growing role in head and neck cancers, particularly cancers of the oropharynx (the tonsils and base of the tongue). A changing disease landscape Head and neck cancers in India have traditionally been linked to tobacco and alcohol use, with a consistent patient profile of older individuals, often with a long history of chewing tobacco, smoking, or heavy drinking. Over the past decade, however, there is a distinct shift. HPV-positive oropharyngeal cancers are being diagnosed at younger ages, with different sexual practices and multiple sexual partners. The disease biology is quite different from that of its tobacco-related counterpart in terms of better response to radiation and chemotherapy. Many HPV-positive patients also have concurrent tobacco or alcohol habits, suggesting dual risk factors rather than a clean substitution of one cause for another. What remains consistent, though, is the clinical relevance of HPV-positive oropharyngeal cancers that tends to respond better to treatment and carry a markedly better prognosis, 5-year survival rates exceeding 80 percent in HPV-positive cases, compared to under 50 percent in HPV-negative disease. Why it matters for India India already carries one of the world’s highest burdens of head and neck cancers, driven largely by tobacco use. In addition HPV positivity is increasing in India over time and its very important to prevent further rise. Layered onto this is a meaningful and measurable HPV contribution, one that gets more complicated when you look closer at the regional picture. A meta-analysis published in BMC Infectious Diseases found that HPV prevalence in head and neck cancers in Eastern India runs nearly 2.5 times higher than in the Westiii. This probably corresponds to higher occurrence of head and neck cancers in the east. It points to something we still don’t fully understand HPV’s role in Indian head and neck cancers isn’t uniform, and our current data is too fragmented to tell us why. This information helps us to identify the areas where we need to focus regarding early diagnosis and prevention. Bridging the gaps Closing this gap isn’t about one intervention, it requires movement on several fronts at once: A patient with a persistent sore throat, an unexplained lump in the neck, or something unusual in the tonsils shouldn’t be dismissed simply because they don’t fit the “typical” head and neck cancer profile, particularly in regions where we already know HPV prevalence runs high. HPV testing, where clinically appropriate, should be integrated into diagnostic workups for oropharyngeal cancers, helping both with prognostication and treatment planning. Clinician awareness needs deliberate reinforcement. Many primary care physicians and even some specialists continue to associate HPV almost exclusively with gynaecological disease. Continuing medical education must catch up with the evolving evidence base. Multidisciplinary care pathways, involving oncologists, ENT surgeons, radiation oncologists, and pathologists are essential to ensure that once identified, HPV-positive cases are managed with an approach suited to their distinct biology and prognosis, while accounting for the dual-risk-factor reality common in Indian patients. Prevention beyond cervical cancer Reframing HPV vaccination as a broader cancer prevention strategy, rather than a gender-specific intervention, could meaningfully expand its public health impact and address a population at risk that current campaigns largely not covering. The way forward As India continues its efforts against viral and vaccine-preventable diseases, HPV-related head and neck cancers deserve further research in terms of actual assessment of the existing regional burden identification, dedicated research into better understanding of the strains involved, wider vaccination efforts and analysis of the outcome of treatment of HPV associated oropharyngeal cancers. Unlike cervical cancer where time tested screening methods are available, screening for these cancers are difficult and hence prevention should be of topmost priority. Vaccination schedules are similar to that for cervical cancer and requires to be implemented across the country especially for high risk susceptible population. References i https://doi.org/10.3390/pathogens14090880 ii https://doi.org/10.1186/s12879-024-09357-2 iii https://doi.org/10.1186/s12879-024-09357-2 head and neck cancersHealthcareHPVoropharyngeal cancers Guest Blogs News Share Related Posts Robotic joint replacement in India: Where technology improves precision and where surgeon judgement remains decisive Lord’s Mark industries receives license for gut microbiome analytics platform Cloudnine and BAPIO Training Academy join hands to support doctors pursuing MRCOG and MRCPCH SSO Cancer Hospitals launches head and neck cancer CoE The new growth playbook for hospitals: Integrated care ecosystems Innvolution Healthcare expands cath lab business into Indonesia AIG Hospitals brings surgical intelligence into the operating room to shape the next-gen of surgical care Comments (0) Add Comment
View five news elements
Time
Published on 12 August 2026; event described as a contemporary public-health discussion with no discrete outbreak date.
Location and population
India; national scope with regional variation (Eastern India vs Western India); affected population includes head and neck cancer patients and clinicians.
Event
A commentary by a senior radiation oncologist highlights the rising burden and distinct epidemiology of HPV-related head and neck cancers in India, emphasizing gaps in prevention, diagnosis, and clinician awareness.
Scale, impact and risk
Globally, HPV-related head and neck cancers have increased by more than 225% over the past 50 years; in India, pooled HPV prevalence in head and neck cancers is 33% with regional variation (47% in Eastern India, under 20% in Western India); HPV-positive oropharyngeal cancers show better prognosis (5-year survival >80%) than HPV-negative cases (<50%).
Response, status and uncertainty
Calls for integrating HPV testing into diagnostic workups for oropharyngeal cancers, strengthening clinician education, adopting multidisciplinary care pathways, and expanding HPV vaccination beyond cervical cancer prevention; regional burden remains insufficiently characterized and research gaps persist.

万泰生物:九价HPV疫苗泰国获批 实现首份境外上市许可 - 东方财富

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)
新闻简报万泰生物全资子公司于2026年8月11日宣布,其九价人乳头瘤病毒(HPV)疫苗已获得泰国食品药品监督管理局批准,获得该产品首个海外上市许可。疫苗 在中国开发,针对 9-45 岁女性,涵盖高风险 HPV 16、18、31、33、45、52、58 型以及低风险 6 型和 11 型。监管许可使万泰能够将 疫苗 出口到泰国,并为在东南亚更广泛的扩张奠定了基础,有可能减少 HPV 相关疾病负担该地区。没有报告病例数、死亡或安全问题,批准过程被描述为完整,不存在任何不确定性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026-08-11(当地时间18:21)。该公司的公告和泰国食品药品监督管理局的批准均于2026年8月11日晚报道。
地点与对象
中国万泰生物科技(厦门万泰沧海生物科技有限公司)开发的疫苗获得泰国食品药品监督管理局的批准。九价 HPV 疫苗 适用于 9-45 岁的女性。
事件
万泰生物宣布,其国产九价人乳头瘤病毒(HPV)疫苗获得泰国FDA监管批准,这是该产品首个海外上市许可。该信息来源于公司官方公告,《中国证券报》东方财富网报道。
规模、影响与风险
没有报告病例或死亡人数。此次批准使 疫苗 能够出口到泰国,扩大了万泰在东南亚的市场份额,并有可能预防目标女性人群中 HPV 16/18/31/33/45/52/58 和 6/11 型的感染。没有提供具体的风险声明。
应对、状态与不确定性
泰国监管审查已完成;疫苗 现已获授权在该地区进行商业发行。该公司计划利用该许可证开展销售并进一步扩展到其他东南亚市场。消息来源中没有提到任何突出的不确定性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

Original title (Chinese)
万泰生物:九价HPV疫苗泰国获批 实现首份境外上市许可 - 东方财富
News Brief
Wan‐Tai Biotech’s wholly‐owned subsidiary announced on 11 August 2026 that its nine‐valent human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine has received approval from Thailand’s Food and Drug Administration, securing the first overseas marketing licence for the product. The vaccine, developed in China, targets females aged 9‐45 and covers high‐risk HPV types 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58 as well as low‐risk types 6 and 11. The regulatory clearance enables Wan‐Tai to export the vaccine to Thailand and lays the groundwork for broader expansion across Southeast Asia, potentially reducing HPV‐related disease burden in the region. No case numbers, deaths, or safety concerns were reported, and the approval process is described as complete with no remaining uncertainties.

View five news elements
Time
The article was published on 2026-08-11 (18:21 local time). The company’s announcement and the Thai Food and Drug Administration’s approval were both reported on the evening of 11 August 2026.
Location and population
The approval was granted by Thailand’s Food and Drug Administration for a vaccine developed by China’s Wan‐Tai Biotech (Xiamen Wan‐Tai Canghai Biotech Co.). The nine‐valent HPV vaccine is intended for females aged 9‐45 years.
Event
Wan‐Tai Biotech announced that its domestically‐developed nine‐valent human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine received regulatory approval from the Thai FDA, marking the first overseas marketing licence for the product. The information comes from the company’s official notice and is reported by China Securities Journal via Eastmoney.
Scale, impact and risk
No case or death numbers are reported. The approval enables export of the vaccine to Thailand, expanding Wan‐Tai’s market presence in Southeast Asia and potentially preventing infections from HPV types 16/18/31/33/45/52/58 and 6/11 in the target female population. No specific risk statements are provided.
Response, status and uncertainty
Regulatory review in Thailand is complete; the vaccine is now authorized for commercial distribution there. The company plans to use the licence to launch sales and further expand into other Southeast Asian markets. No outstanding uncertainties are mentioned in the source.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

为什么 HPV 疫苗接种 对男孩和女孩都很重要

Why HPV Vaccination Matters for Boys as Well as Girls
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 14 日发表在《JAMA Oncology》上的一项研究发现,HPV 疫苗接种 与男性患某些 HPV 相关癌症的风险降低 46% 相关,包括头颈癌、食道癌、肛门癌和阴茎癌。该研究包括 615,155 名已接种疫苗的男性和 2,290,623 名年龄在 9 至 26 岁之间的未接种疫苗的男性,并对结果进行了长达 10 年的跟踪。研究结果表明,HPV 疫苗接种 可能为两性提供更广泛的癌症预防益处,将话题从 宫颈癌 预防转向更具包容性的方法。然而,该研究是观察性的,这种关联并不能证明因果关系。结果支持将 HPV 疫苗接种 作为性别中立癌症预防策略的一部分,强调其对于男孩和年轻男性在减轻 HPV 相关疾病负担方面的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于 2026 年 8 月 14 日发表,并对结果进行了长达 10 年的跟踪。证据没有指定确切的事件日期。
地点与对象
该研究纳入了来自未指定地点的 9 至 26 岁男性,分析了 615,155 名接种疫苗的男性和 2,290,623 名未接种疫苗的男性。
事件
《JAMA Oncology》上发表的一项研究发现,HPV 疫苗接种 与男性患某些 HPV 相关癌症(包括头颈癌、食道癌、肛门癌和阴茎癌)的风险较低之间存在关联。研究结果表明,HPV 疫苗接种 可能对男女都有更广泛的癌症预防益处。
规模、影响与风险
接种疫苗的组发生 HPV 相关癌症的风险降低了 46%。该研究并未发现 疫苗接种 消除癌症风险,但发现 疫苗接种 与降低癌症 发病率 之间存在关联。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是观察性的,证据表明研究结果应被理解为关联性而不是因果关系的证明。分析正在进行中,研究结果支持对 HPV 疫苗接种 进行更全面的理解。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Why HPV Vaccination Matters for Boys as Well as Girls

News Brief
A study published in JAMA Oncology on August 14, 2026, found that HPV vaccination is associated with a 46% lower risk of certain HPV-related cancers in males, including head and neck, esophageal, anal, and penile cancers. The research included 615,155 vaccinated males and 2,290,623 unvaccinated males aged 9 to 26 years, with outcomes followed for up to 10 years. The findings suggest that HPV vaccination may offer broader cancer-prevention benefits for both sexes, shifting the conversation from cervical cancer prevention to a more inclusive approach. However, the study is observational, and the association does not prove causation. The results support considering HPV vaccination as part of a sex-neutral cancer prevention strategy, emphasizing its importance for boys and young men in reducing the burden of HPV-related diseases.

MEDINDIA MEDINDIA Consult Online Doctor Courses Explore Healthy Living News Health A-Z Calculators Articles Drugs Directories Education More Search Medindia Medindia » News » Health In Focus » Why HPV Vaccination Matters for Boys as Well as Girls Why HPV Vaccination Matters for Boys as Well as Girls Written by Dr. Vasantha Medically Reviewed by Colleen Fleiss M.Sc on Aug 14 2026 3:20 PM HPV vaccination may have a broader role in protecting boys and young men from certain HPV-related cancers. Highlights: HPV vaccination was linked to a lower risk of HPV-related cancers in males Male cancer prevention gains importance as HPV vaccination benefits extend beyond cervical cancer HPV-related cancers studied included head and neck, esophageal, anal and penile cancers HPV vaccination is often discussed in connection with cervical cancer, but a new finding highlights why the vaccine also matters for boys and young men. A study is shifting the conversation from HPV vaccination as a women’s health measure to broader cancer prevention for both sexes (1✔ ✔Trusted Source Nine-Valent Human Papillomavirus Vaccination and Related Cancers in Males Go to source). A study published in JAMA Oncology found that vaccination was linked to a lower risk of several HPV-related cancers in males. The analysis included 615,155 vaccinated males and 2,290,623 unvaccinated males aged 9 to 26 years. After matching the groups, more than 510,000 males were included in each group, with outcomes followed for up to 10 years. The vaccinated group had a 46% lower risk of the combined HPV-related cancer outcome. Human Papillomavirus Infection - Risk Factors, Symptoms, Screening, Treatment & Prevention Human papillomavirus (HPV) infection, depending on the human papillomavirus that causes it, can result in common warts or can cause cervical cancer. HPV Vaccination And Cancer Risk In Males The researchers looked at cancers linked to HPV in four areas: Head and neck Esophagus Anus Penis For someone deciding whether an adolescent boy should receive the 9-valent HPV vaccine, the findings add evidence that the potential benefits extend beyond the health concerns traditionally associated with girls and women. The study did not find that vaccination eliminates cancer risk. Instead, it found an association between vaccination and lower cancer incidence, making the distinction important when interpreting the findings. HPV Infection : Two-Dose Vaccine Found Effective In Treating Genital Warts Genital warts occur as a result of Human Papillomavirus infections. Two doses of HPV vaccine could be effective in treating genital warts. HPV Vaccine Benefits For Boys And Young Men The lower cancer incidence was seen among both younger males aged 9 to 14 years and those aged 15 to 26 years. This suggests that the observed association was not limited to one age group within the population studied. For a family discussing routine healthcare for a school-age boy, HPV vaccination can therefore be viewed as part of a broader approach to protecting against HPV-related disease, rather than as a vaccine relevant only to girls. Test Your Knowledge on Human Papillomavirus (HPV) Human papillomavirus (HPV) infection is the most common sexually transmitted infection spread by person to person contact, mainly sexual intercourse and affecting the skin and genital areas. There are over 100 types of HPV and some types can cause ... Why HPV Vaccination Matters Beyond Cervical Cancer HPV-related cancer can affect people irrespective of sex. The findings are particularly relevant because vaccination programmes have often been discussed through the lens of cervical cancer prevention. So, sex-neutral HPV vaccination can have relevance for male health as well. Therefore, "the 9v-HPV vaccine is beneficial not only for females but also for males," according to Taito Kitano and Sayaka Yoshida. Travel vaccination calculator guides travelers on information about travel to 247 countries. Safe travel is the first step to enjoy your holiday or business ... What The HPV Vaccine Findings Mean For Families Consider a parent reviewing recommended vaccinations for a son during adolescence. The decision is no longer only about preventing an infection that may affect someone else later in life. The evidence indicates that vaccination may also be associated with a lower risk of certain cancers affecting males. The study also included young men aged 15 to 26 years. However, this observational evidence should be understood as an association rather than proof that vaccination directly caused the lower cancer risk. HPV Vaccination And Sex-Neutral Cancer Prevention The broader significance lies in how HPV vaccination is viewed. Cancer prevention is not exclusively a women's health issue, and the findings support considering HPV vaccination within a sex-neutral prevention strategy. The evidence does not establish protection against every HPV-related cancer or disease. It specifically examined the combined occurrence of head and neck, esophageal, anal and penile cancers among the male population studied. Going forward, these findings add support to a more inclusive understanding of HPV vaccination, where protecting boys and young men is also considered part of reducing the burden of HPV-related cancers. For families making vaccination decisions, understanding who can benefit from HPV prevention can make conversations with healthcare professionals more informed and meaningful. Frequently Asked Questions Q: What types of cancer were linked to HPV vaccination in males? A: The study examined four cancer groups associated with human papillomavirus (HPV): head and neck cancers, esophageal cancer, anal cancer and penile cancer. Researchers assessed whether males who received the 9-valent HPV vaccine had a different risk of developing these cancers compared with unvaccinated males. Q: Does the HPV vaccine reduce cancer risk in males? A: The study found an association between HPV vaccination and a lower risk of the combined HPV-related cancer outcome in males. This adds to evidence that HPV vaccination may provide important cancer-prevention benefits for males as well as females. However, an observational study showing an association does not by itself prove that vaccination directly caused the lower cancer risk. Q: Why is HPV vaccination important for boys and young men? A: HPV can infect both males and females and is associated with several cancers. Vaccinating boys and young men can help protect them against HPV infections that may later contribute to cancer. It can also contribute to broader HPV prevention by reducing transmission within the population. Q: At what age were males included in the HPV vaccine study? A: The study included males aged 9 to 26 years who had either received the 9-valent HPV vaccine or remained unvaccinated. Researchers compared cancer outcomes between the vaccinated and unvaccinated groups. Q: What is the 9-valent HPV vaccine? A: The 9-valent HPV vaccine protects against nine types of HPV that are associated with HPV-related diseases and cancers. It is designed to provide broader protection against HPV types than earlier HPV vaccines. Q: Does the HPV vaccine protect males against every type of cancer? A: No. HPV vaccination does not prevent every form of cancer. The study specifically examined head and neck, esophageal, anal and penile cancers in relation to HPV vaccination. The vaccine is intended to prevent infection with specific HPV types and, in turn, reduce the risk of diseases associated with those infections. Q: Can HPV cause cancer in men? A: Yes. HPV infection can contribute to several cancers in males, including anal, penile and certain head and neck cancers. Most HPV infections do not lead to cancer, but persistent infection with certain high-risk HPV types can cause cellular changes that may eventually become cancerous. Q: Does HPV vaccination work only when given before HPV exposure? A: HPV vaccines work best when administered before exposure to HPV, which is why vaccination is recommended at younger ages. The vaccine prevents new HPV infections but does not treat an HPV infection or cancer that is already present. Q: Does this study prove that HPV vaccination prevents cancer in males? A: The findings provide evidence of a lower observed risk of the combined HPV-related cancer outcome among vaccinated males, but the study design should be considered when interpreting the results. An association between vaccination and lower cancer incidence does not necessarily establish direct causation on its own. Q: Should parents consider HPV vaccination for boys? A: The findings add to the evidence supporting HPV vaccination for males, particularly because HPV can cause cancers in men as well as women. Parents should discuss the appropriate vaccination schedule and individual circumstances with a qualified healthcare professional. Time
The study was published on August 14, 2026, and the outcomes were followed for up to 10 years. The evidence does not specify an exact event date.
Location and population
The study included males aged 9 to 26 years from unspecified locations, with 615,155 vaccinated males and 2,290,623 unvaccinated males in the analysis.
Event
A study published in JAMA Oncology found an association between HPV vaccination and a lower risk of certain HPV-related cancers in males, including head and neck, esophageal, anal, and penile cancers. The findings suggest that HPV vaccination may have broader cancer-prevention benefits for both sexes.
Scale, impact and risk
The vaccinated group had a 46% lower risk of the combined HPV-related cancer outcome. The study did not find that vaccination eliminates cancer risk, but it found an association between vaccination and lower cancer incidence.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study is observational, and the evidence indicates that the findings should be understood as an association rather than proof of causation. The analysis is ongoing, and the study's results support a more inclusive understanding of HPV vaccination.
