头颈癌最终 监测 治疗后的 监测 护理费用:监测、监测 和最终结果 (SEER)-医疗保险分析 - cureus.com

Costs of Surveillance Care After Definitive Treatment of Head and Neck Cancer: A Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER)-Medicare Analysis - cureus.com
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 10 日发布的 SEER-Medicare 分析量化了 13,339 名 65 岁及以上美国医疗保险受益人在 2010 年至 2017 年间接受非转移性头颈鳞状细胞癌治疗的常规 监测 费用。监测 期间的付款人支出中位数为 1,246 美元,但费用有所倾斜,最高超过 7,600 美元的 5%。监测 在 HPV 阳性口咽癌的年轻患者和随访时间较长的患者中最为昂贵。软喉镜费用占大部分。该研究在 ASTRO 2025 上发表,建立了一个基线来评估新兴的 HPV 驱动的复发检测是否可以通过实现早期检测来减少成像和内窥镜检查的支出。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 10 日发布;研究期为 2010 年至 2017 年,医疗保险索赔截至 2019 年;监测 时期定义为 治疗 后 90 天至死亡、临终关怀入组或其他癌症 治疗。
地点与对象
美国;年龄≥65岁患有头颈部非转移性鳞状细胞癌(口腔、鼻旁窦、咽、喉)的医疗保险受益人;口咽最常见部位;33%的口咽癌患者HPV呈阳性,55%的HPV状态未知。
事件
SEER-Medicare 分析量化了前生物标志物时代 治疗 监测 后常规护理的付款人成本,为新兴 HPV 驱动的复发检测的成本效益评估提供信息。
规模、影响与风险
分析了 13,339 名患者;监测 的中位成本为 1,246 美元(IQR 420 美元–2,381 美元);前 5% 的收入为 7,657 美元(IQR 6,656-9,062 美元);年轻 HPV 阳性口咽癌患者和随访时间较长的患者的费用最高;软喉镜收费最频繁。
应对、状态与不确定性
正在调查中;基线成本量化完成;没有临床干预报告;在 ASTRO 2025 上公布的研究结果;生物标志物替代与成像的未来成本效益模型待定;55% 的口咽部病例缺乏 HPV 状态数据。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Costs of Surveillance Care After Definitive Treatment of Head and Neck Cancer: A Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER)-Medicare Analysis - cureus.com

News Brief
A SEER-Medicare analysis published 10 August 2026 quantified routine surveillance costs for 13,339 U.S. Medicare beneficiaries aged 65 and older treated for non-metastatic head and neck squamous cell carcinoma between 2010 and 2017. Median payer spending during surveillance was $1,246, but costs were skewed, with the top 5% exceeding $7,600. Surveillance was most expensive among younger patients with HPV-positive oropharyngeal cancer and those followed longer. Flexible laryngoscopy accounted for the majority of charges. The study, presented at ASTRO 2025, establishes a baseline to evaluate whether emerging HPV-driven recurrence assays could reduce imaging and endoscopy spending by enabling earlier detection.
Original source text (English)
Abstract Introduction: Following curative treatment of squamous carcinoma of the head and neck, surveillance care is indicated to detect recurrence. Historically, physical exams are the backbone of surveillance, but serial imaging and flexible endoscopy are also utilized. Recently, assays have become commercially available, which could aid in early detection of recurrence. To understand the potential economic impact of these assays, a baseline of surveillance costs must be established. We performed a Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER)-Medicare analysis to quantify the costs of classic surveillance procedures. Materials and methods: We obtained data from the 2010-2017 SEER program linked to traditional Medicare Part A & B claims and enrollment information dating 2010-2019. Patients with non-metastatic cancers of the head and neck, including oral cavity, paranasal sinus, pharynx, and larynx, were identified. Definitive treatment with either surgery or radiation therapy (with or without systemic therapy) was required for inclusion. Patients were excluded if they had any lapse in insurance coverage (i.e., Parts A & B) or were enrolled in Medicare Advantage. The surveillance period was defined from 90 days past treatment through the date of death, hospice enrollment, or additional cancer treatments. Procedures related to routine surveillance were identified, including flexible endoscopy and imaging. The cumulative Medicare spend was quantified, adjusting for inflation to 2021 U.S. dollars. Results: In total, 13,339 patients were identified; all were age ≥ 65. The most common site was the oropharynx (OPC), followed by larynx cancers. Of the OPC patients, 33% were human papillomavirus positive (HPV+), and for 55%, the HPV status was unknown. The median clinical follow-up was 43.0 months, and the median surveillance period was 16.6 months. The median costs were $1,246 (IQR $420-$2,381), with the top 5% incurring a cost of $7,657 (IQR $6,656-9,062). Surveillance was more expensive among younger, HPV+ OPC patients and for those who followed longer. Flexible laryngoscopy was the most frequent charge. Conclusions: In an older population, payer costs follow a wide and skewed distribution but are highest among younger HPV+ OPC patients. These findings facilitate cost-effectiveness assessment of novel assays. Opportunity exists to reduce imaging and endoscopy spend by substituting more accurate biomarkers. Introduction Head and neck cancer (HNC) presents a significant global health concern, accounting for over 790,000 new cases annually [1]. Despite therapeutic advances, recurrence rates remain high: up to 40% of patients experience disease recurrence after definitive treatment [2, 3]. Recurrence risk is highest in the initial post-treatment period and is influenced by factors such as tumor stage, human papillomavirus (HPV) status, and primary site [4]. Current surveillance strategies for HNC survivors are guided by consensus recommendations from organizations such as the National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN), American Cancer Society (ACS), and the American College of Radiology (ACR), which advocate for regular history and physical examination, with imaging reserved for symptomatic patients or those with abnormal findings [5-8]. The recommended frequency of clinical follow-up is every one to three months in the first year, every two to six months in the second year, and less frequently thereafter. However, the utility of routine imaging in asymptomatic patients is increasingly questioned, as multiple studies have failed to demonstrate a survival benefit or improved salvage rates with scheduled imaging compared to clinical examination alone [2, 9-11]. Routine surveillance imaging may contribute to unnecessary financial burden, radiation exposure, and resource utilization without clear evidence of benefit. Therefore, recent guidelines suggest that surveillance should be individualized based on recurrence risk, with only higher-risk patients for whom the tumor site is difficult to visualize considered for serial imaging [5, 12]. Moving beyond serial imaging, emerging evidence now supports the use of circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA) and tumor tissue-modified viral (TTMV)-DNA assays as highly sensitive and specific tools for the early detection of recurrence, particularly in HPV-driven oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma (OPSCC) [13-15]. These assays can be performed serially and have demonstrated high positive and negative predictive values, often identifying recurrence months before clinical or radiologic detection [16]. While promising, concerns over the costs of serial measurement have slowed the adoption of these biomarkers [17]. Initial cost-effectiveness modeling suggests that strict adherence to biomarkers instead of imaging may be economically feasible [17, 18]. However, the validity of any cost-effectiveness model is contingent upon the baseline costs of the reference (traditional surveillance modalities), which have not been well quantified. Herein, we used the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER)-Medicare database to quantify the costs of surveillance care in the pre-biomarker era, providing a foundation for a value assessment of novel assays used in survivorship. This work was presented in digital poster format at the 2025 American Society of Radiation Oncology (ASTRO) meeting. Materials & Methods Data source Formed in 1973, the SEER program is a set of population-based registries that was expanded from 18 to 21 geographical areas in 2018 and covers approximately 35% of the United States population [19]. Through a de-identified process, approximately 96% of cancer cases held in the SEER database are linked to Medicare claims data, forming a relational database queryable for cost of care. For this study, files obtained included (1) SEER Cancer Data, Medicare Enrollment Master Beneficiary Summary File (MBSF); (3) Medicare Provider Analysis and Review (MEDPAR) file; (4) National Claims History (NCH) file; (5) Outpatient file; and (6) Hospice file. Atrium Health Institutional Review Board approval was waived, and a Data Use Agreement was executed. This report conforms to STrengthening the Reporting of OBservational studies in Epidemiology (STROBE) guidelines for observational research [20]. Study cohort We obtained data from the 2010-2017 SEER program linked to traditional Medicare Part A & B claims and enrollment information dating from 2010-2019. Patients age 65 and older, with a diagnosis date of cancer from 2010 to 2017 with non-metastatic, pathologically confirmed cases of squamous cell carcinoma originating from mucosal subsites of the head and neck, including oral cavity, paranasal sinus, pharynx, and larynx, were identified. Patients were excluded if they had any lapse in insurance coverage (i.e., Parts A & B) or were enrolled in Medicare Advantage (Part C). Outcomes Healthcare Common Procedure Coding System (HCPCS) codes were utilized to differentiate the phases of care, defined as treatment, surveillance, and recurrence [21]. The “surveillance” period was defined from 90 days past treatment through the date of death, hospice enrollment, or additional cancer treatments. Treatment was defined using HCPCS codes related to surgical, radiation, or chemotherapy treatments (Table 1). Note that oral chemotherapies (filed under Medicare Part D) were not included due to a limited role in the management of head and neck cancer. Costs The primary outcome was the cumulative costs of surveillance-related procedures. Procedures related to routine surveillance were identified, including flexible endoscopy and imaging. Imaging procedures included in the cost analysis are listed in Table 2. The cumulative Medicare payment from these services was quantified by summing all paid claims related to surveillance HCPCS codes with a date of service contained within the defined surveillance period (the period from 90 days post treatment until the next treatment, hospice enrollment, or death). For hospital services, this amount does not include the claim pass-through per diem payments made by Medicare. To obtain the total amount paid by Medicare for the claim, the pass-through amount (which is the daily per diem amount) must be multiplied by the number of Medicare-covered days and added to the claim payment amount (this field), a correction that we performed. For non-hospital (and hospital outpatient) services and for other non-institutional services (carrier and Durable Medical Equipment (DME)), this variable equals the total actual Medicare payment amount, and pass-through amounts do not apply. For Part B non-institutional services (carrier and DME), this variable equals the sum of all the line item-level Medicare payments as listed in the National Claims History file. Claims were adjusted for inflation to 2021 U.S. dollars using the Consumer Price Index (CPI) [22]. Statistical analysis SAS input programs (SAS Institute Inc., Cary, NC, USA) provided by the SEER program were used to import six provided files, which included: (1) SEER Cancer Data, (2) Medicare Enrollment MBSF file, (3) MEDPAR, (4) NCH, (5) Outpatient, and (6) Hospice files. All files were combined by setting type and patient ID. Table 3 presents the full list of studied variable names. We selected claims to be used by starting with only therapy and surveillance claims; no other types of claims were included. We selected only patients in the cohort who had a 90-day window free of treatment, indicating the patient had started a surveillance period for subsequent head and neck cancers. Patients without a surveillance period were counted to have a payment of zero but left in the analysis to ensure an accurate denominator. Analysis of cost patterns examined payments by age, race, ethnicity, diagnosis year, cancer site, and HPV status. Additionally, analyses were performed to assess Medicare payments by HPV status for oropharyngeal cancers alone as well as by secondary cancer treatment (surgery and radiation), and year of surveillance care. Lastly, average frequency of payments for each HCPCS code (n>15) in the surveillance period was analyzed across all patients in the study population. Descriptive statistics are used primarily for the purpose of calculating cumulative payments. No advanced regressions or analyses were utilized. All analyses were performed in SAS 9.4 and R (R Foundation for Statistical Computing, Vienna, Austria). Results In total, 13,339 patients were identified; all were aged ≥65. The most common site was oropharynx (OPC), followed by larynx primaries. Of the OPC patients, 34% were known HPV positive (HPV+). Table 4 displays the characteristics of patients included (patient, disease & treatment factors), and Figure 1 demonstrates the STROBE diagram of the cohort formation. Surveillance period The median clinical follow-up was 43.0 months (IQR 21-69), and the median surveillance period was 16.6 months (IQR 6-36). The most common reasons for discontinuing surveillance were additional treatment or hospice enrollment. Cumulative costs The median costs for the entire cohort were $1,246, and the arithmetic mean was $1,841, with an IQR of $420-$2,381 (Table 5). These costs varied widely and demonstrated a rightward skew, with the top 5% of patients incurring significantly higher costs (median $7,657, IQR $6,656-9,062). Costs differed across demographic groups. Younger patients had higher expenditures, with a median of $1,485 among those aged 65-69 compared to $857 for those aged ≥80. Male patients had slightly higher costs than female patients ($1,323 vs. $1,082). Differences by race were modest, although Asian patients demonstrated somewhat higher median costs ($1,577), while Black patients incurred lower median costs ($1,077). Nasopharyngeal ($2,596), oropharyngeal ($1,763), and hypopharyngeal ($1,674) cancers had the highest median costs, whereas laryngeal cancers had lower costs ($1,029), likely reflecting easier visualization on physical examination. HPV+ oropharyngeal cancer was associated with higher surveillance costs ($1,924, IQR $903-$3,702), consistent with longer survival and extended follow-up in this subgroup. Treatment modality also influenced costs; receipt of radiation therapy was associated with substantially higher expenditures compared to no radiation ($1,878 vs. $703), while surgery was associated with only modest differences ($1,337 vs. $1,235). Temporal patterns demonstrated that surveillance spending was front-loaded. Median costs were highest in the first post-treatment year at $1,069 (IQR $344-$2,001), decreasing to $622 (IQR $277-$1,364) in year 2 and $477 (IQR $218-$1,045) in year 3. Across all groups, flexible laryngoscopy was the most frequently billed service, while imaging, though less frequent, contributed disproportionately to higher cumulative costs, particularly among high-cost outliers. Discussion In this study, we establish a “real-world” baseline for surveillance costs in the era prior to novel biomarkers such as TTMV-ctDNA, using a population-based dataset representative of older adults. We found that a significant rightward skew in costs exists and that compliance with routine surveillance may be limited, with many patients incurring zero costs related to scans or exams. Overall, this is a representative sample that suggests surveillance care is likely less of a financial burden than treatment costs and that novel biomarkers do have the potential to increase Medicare costs as adoption increases. Comparing our estimate to other assessments, our projection appears credible. One study by Mariotto et al. reported “continuing phase” care in 2019 U.S. dollars to be $5,200, including all charges [23]. Our paper limits charges, which may be related to care of head and neck cancer directly; thus, the average estimate seems comparable. In our previous cost-effectiveness analysis, we modeled the Medicare spend over five years to be an estimated $4,760 (2020 US dollars) based on the publicly available Medicare physician fee schedule. In this current report, most “real-world” costs were over the first three years, after which compliance dropped significantly and thus reduced the overall costs [17, 24]. The implications of this analysis are notable for economic evaluations of surveillance protocols. First, "real-world" Medicare spending on surveillance is likely dwarfed by treatment costs, particularly when considering novel pharmaceuticals and costs of hospitalization. Second, compliance with surveillance is likely limited (particularly in an older population), and patients may not receive all the “per protocol” assays beyond two to three years. Finally, these parameters can be used to improve the accuracy of additional cost-effectiveness assessments, which may guide more efficient health policies in the future. There are notable limitations to this study. First, this is an older cohort aged 65 and above, and it is possible that real-world utilization and compliance may be more common in a younger population. Second, the rationale for each HCPCS charge is not available in the data, and some "overcounting" of scan charges is likely. Third, enrollment in Medicare Part C (Medicare Advantage) has increased steadily over the years, threatening the generalizability of the traditional Medicare Part B sample. Additionally, 18.6% of patients did not have any claims, which could represent follow-up not captured or incomplete data. A combination of these limitations of the SEER-Medicare program likely contributed to the short surveillance follow-up (median 16.6 months) despite the longer survival follow-up (median 43.0 months). In other words, the claims data did not fully match the survival data, a limitation of SEER-Medicare. Finally, the study included patients treated between 2010 and 2017. While this was performed to be representative of a population prior to the utilization of novel biomarkers, it is conceivable that practice patterns have changed such that utilization of scans may be higher today. Conclusions In an older population, United States payer spending on surveillance care follows a wide and skewed distribution but is highest among younger HPV-associated OPC patients. Future cost-effectiveness assessments of novel biomarkers can utilize this information, obtained in the pre-ctDNA era, to inform parameter inputs and sensitivity analyses. Opportunity exists to reduce imaging and endoscopy spend by substituting more accurate biomarkers such as ctDNA, though this may increase Medicare expenses overall. Unrestricted serial use of more costly biomarkers may not demonstrate cost-effectiveness compared to the historical standard, given the low costs observed in this study. However, if restricted to a higher-risk population, it may be possible to demonstrate the cost-effectiveness of serial biomarkers. References - Deng M, Lin Y, Yan L, Fei Z, Chen C, Ding J: Global burden of head and neck cancer from 1990 to 2021: a comprehensive analysis and projections to 2030 based on the global burden of disease study 2021. J Clin Oncol. 2003, 21:92-8. 10.1200/JCO.2003.01.008 - Ang KK, Harris J, Wheeler R, et al.: Human papillomavirus and survival of patients with oropharyngeal cancer. N Engl J Med. 2010, 363:24-35. 10.1056/NEJMoa0912217 - Hanna GJ, Chang SS, Siddiqui F, et al.: Imaging and biomarker surveillance for head and neck squamous cell carcinoma: a systematic review and American Radium Society appropriate use criteria statement. 特朗普发布命令更改童年 疫苗 时间表 - 这是医生希望父母了解的内容 - 日常健康

Trump Issues Order to Change the Childhood Vaccine Schedule — Here’s What Doctors Want Parents to Know - Everyday Health
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 11 日,唐纳德·特朗普总统在美国签署了一项行政命令,显着改变了儿童 疫苗接种 时间表。该命令将推荐的 疫苗 数量从 CDC 目前确定的 17 种减少到 11 种,其中包括针对所有儿童的推荐 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV)。主要变化包括提议将麻疹、腮腺炎和风疹 (MMR) 疫苗 分成三种单独的单一疾病疫苗,以及对几种 疫苗 实施“共享临床决策”,这将默认情况改为不接种疫苗,除非父母特别要求。美国医学会和美国儿科学会等主要医疗机构批评了该命令,认为该命令缺乏科学证据,可能会削弱人们对免疫接种的信心,从而危及公众健康。虽然该命令要求改进对 疫苗 时间和安全性的研究,但医学专家坚持认为,当前的时间表得到了广泛的科学数据的支持。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该行政命令于 2026 年 8 月 11 日星期一签署;该报告发布于2026年8月11日(标题中注明为2026年8月12日)[N15]。
地点与对象
美国;特别影响儿童和青少年[N15,N30]。
事件
唐纳德·特朗普总统签署了一项行政命令,对美国儿童 疫苗接种 时间表进行重大修改 [N15]。该命令建议 疫苗 针对 11 种可预防疾病,而不是 CDC 目前确定的 17 种[N16]。它还建议将 MMR 疫苗 分成三个单独的疫苗,并为某些免疫接种引入“共享临床决策”[N17、N20、N22]。
规模、影响与风险
该命令将推荐的 疫苗 数量从 17 减少到 11 [N16]。它将 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 分类为推荐给所有儿童的 疫苗 [N20]。医学专家警告说,在没有可靠证据的情况下改变经过验证的时间表可能会削弱公众信心并使儿童的健康面临风险[N19]。此外,该命令强调各州必须提供免疫接种的宗教和医疗豁免[N30]。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生与公众服务部安全儿童 疫苗 工作组已被要求提出计划,以改进 疫苗 关于顺序、安全性和时间安排的研究 [N29]。美国儿科学会 (AAP) 表示不会根据该命令更改其 疫苗 建议 [N36]。对于这些变化的科学依据,专业人士存在明显的反对意见[N14,N42]。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Trump Issues Order to Change the Childhood Vaccine Schedule — Here’s What Doctors Want Parents to Know - Everyday Health

News Brief
On August 11, 2026, President Donald Trump signed an executive order in the United States that significantly alters the childhood vaccination schedule. The order reduces the number of recommended vaccines from the 17 currently identified by the CDC to 11, including human papillomavirus (HPV) as a recommendation for all children. Key changes include the proposal to split the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine into three separate single-disease shots and the implementation of 'shared clinical decision-making' for several vaccines, which shifts the default to not vaccinating unless specifically requested by parents. Major medical bodies, such as the American Medical Association and the American Academy of Pediatrics, have criticized the order, arguing it lacks scientific evidence and could jeopardize public health by weakening confidence in immunization. While the order calls for improved research into vaccine timing and safety, medical experts maintain that the current schedule is supported by extensive scientific data.
Original source text (English)

Health Effects, Risk Factors for Exposure, and More 3Microplastics in Bottled Water Are More Abundant Than Previously Thought SEE MORE News Trump Issues Order to Change the Childhood Vaccine Schedule — Here’s What Doctors Want Parents to Know Major medical groups say the executive order isn’t based on science and would leave children vulnerable to preventable illnesses. iStock By Korin Miller Published on August 11, 2026 Fact-Checked by Emily Kay Votruba Add us on Google President Donald Trump signed a controversial executive order Monday that makes major changes to childhood vaccinations in the United States. [1] The executive order recommends vaccines against 11 preventable diseases, instead of the 17 currently identified by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). The plan also calls for splitting up the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine (currently given as a single shot) into three separate vaccines, and stresses “shared decision-making” when considering immunizations like the flu shot. [1] Multiple major medical organizations and lawmakers have spoken out against the changes. “Altering a proven vaccination schedule without credible evidence risks weakening public confidence and putting children’s health at risk,” said Willie Underwood III, MD, MPH, the president of the American Medical Association, in a statement shared with Everyday Health. What the Executive Order Said The executive order split its vaccine recommendations into categories: Vaccines recommended for all children: Measles, mumps, rubella, diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis, polio, Haemophilus influenzae type B, pneumococcal disease, human papillomavirus, and varicella (chicken pox) Recommended for “certain high-risk groups or populations”: Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) monoclonal antibodies, hepatitis A, hepatitis B, meningococcal B, meningococcal ACWY, and dengue Vaccines that should be given only with shared clinical decision-making: Hepatitis A, hepatitis B, rotavirus, meningococcal disease, influenza, and COVID-19. Shared clinical-decision making means the default is not to vaccinate, and parents have to specifically request these vaccines. [2] The order also recommends splitting the combined MMR vaccine into “three separate single-disease shots” — an option not currently available in the United States. “To the maximum extent feasible, all childhood immunizations should be administered at separate medical visits,” the order states. The order cites vaccine guidance from “peer nations” as part of the rationale for the changes, which Amesh A. Adalja, MD, a senior scholar at the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security in Baltimore, calls an “interesting” claim. “Peer nations, that the administration often cherry-picks, all use the MMR,” he says. One exception is Japan, he notes, which uses a measles-rubella vaccine and gives a mumps vaccine separately. [3] Finally, the order called on the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services Task Force on Safer Childhood Vaccines to present plans to the president to “improve” vaccine research related to the sequence, safety, and timing of childhood vaccines. It stressed that states have “obligations to provide religious and medical exemptions from childhood and adolescent immunization requirements,” and threatens "appropriate action” if these are not granted. Ultimately, though, only the states have the authority to determine which vaccinations are required for school attendance, not the federal government, notes Dr. Adalja. Trump Offers Disproven Autism Link as Rationale for Changes Although it was not mentioned in the executive order itself, President Trump repeatedly suggested in a press conference that childhood vaccines and their timing could play a role in rising autism rates — a claim disproven by decades of research. [4] “Dozens of studies involving millions of people show there is no link between vaccines and autism, and yet federal leaders continue to promote this outdated, disproven idea to scare families,” said Andrew D. Racine, MD, PhD, the president of the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP), in a statement. [5] “Today’s executive order will do nothing to support families of children with autism or advance understanding of the condition,” he added. AAP said it will not change its vaccine recommendations in response to the executive order. Senator Bill Cassidy, the Republican chair of the Senate health committee, issued his own statement on X. “I’m a doctor. This executive order is wrong. The President does not have the expertise to make these changes,” he wrote. “Vaccines are overwhelmingly safe. Vaccines are effective. Vaccines DO NOT cause autism.” [6] Doctors Oppose the Recommendations for Many Reasons Experts say their biggest concern about the executive order is that it isn’t based on science. “We know that the current childhood vaccination schedule is supported by years of scientific evidence. These new recommendations are not,” says Jennifer M. Walsh, DNP, an assistant professor at George Washington University School of Nursing in Ashburn, Virginia, and a primary care pediatric nurse practitioner. As for dividing the MMR shot into three separate vaccines administered in separate medical visits, “Spacing them out doesn’t make any sense to reduce the burden on the immune system,” Walsh says. She points out that time spent on the playground can expose children to more antigens (substances that can trigger an immune response) than vaccination does. Besides, the combination shot is safe, Adalja says. “The combined shot exists because giving three antigens at once is exactly as safe as giving them separately,” he says. The changes would pose logistical and cost issues for families, too, Walsh says. “Spacing vaccines out leads to more incomplete vaccinations for kids,” she says. “It’s also a higher cost, and more of a burden on parents and healthcare practices because you have to have the staff come in for those multiple visits.” Parents will have to take more time off from work for all those vaccination visits, which can lead to a drop in earnings, and they may need to take additional time off if their child develops a fever or other reaction to the vaccines, she says. Other countries may also have different childhood vaccination schedules for a reason, Walsh adds. “They are different countries with different healthcare access and infrastructure,” she says. “It is not comparing apples to apples.” Order Unlikely to Impact Health Insurance Coverage Health insurers follow recommendations from the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP) when deciding on coverage, Adalja notes, and these guidelines predate the Trump administration. As a result, the executive order “will not have any impact on insurance coverage.” Consult With Your Healthcare Provider, Experts Say Walsh stresses the importance of getting vaccination advice from medical providers, not politicians. “Talk to your pediatric provider about vaccine recommendations,” she says. “We want to talk to parents about any concerns they have. It is our life mission to educate about why we do this, and to help kids thrive.” Adalja also recommends talking to your healthcare provider. “Follow the schedule your pediatrician and the American Academy of Pediatrics still publish independently of this order — that’s the one built from data, rather than politics,” he says. PDS Biotechnology 停止 PDS0101 试验,转向 PDS0301 治疗结直肠癌 - 临床试验先锋

PDS Biotechnology Halts PDS0101 Trial, Pivots to PDS0301 in Colorectal Cancer - The Clinical Trial Vanguard
新闻简报PDS Biotechnology 已停止 PDS0101(一种治疗 HPV16 阳性头颈鳞状细胞癌的 治疗)的 3 期 VERSATILE-003 试验,并将重点转移到治疗转移性结直肠癌的 PDS0301。这一决定在 8 月 11 日的股东信中宣布,反映了公司对资源的战略优先顺序,以寻求潜在的更具差异化的机会。VERSATILE-002 2 期试验显示,与 pembrolizumab 联合使用,中位总生存期为 39.3 个月,但在 3 期之前停止意味着该数据无法成熟为标签声明。该公司现在押注于PDS0301,这是一种IL-12免疫细胞因子,旨在靶向肿瘤并减少全身毒性。然而,PDS0301 的成功取决于在治疗剂量下表现出完全的耐受性,这仍然不确定。这一枢纽标志着重点从 HPV16 相关癌症转向结直肠癌,而免疫疗法的渗透仍然是一个挑战。该决定是在试验停止的同时披露的,因此更换策略没有任何含糊之处。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该公司于2026年8月11日宣布暂停PDS0101试验,该决定与VERSATILE-003试验暂停同时披露。活动日期为2026年8月11日,发布日期为2026年8月12日。
地点与对象
此次活动涉及美国公司 PDS Biotechnology 及其针对 HPV16 阳性头颈鳞状细胞癌和转移性结直肠癌的临床试验。
事件
PDS Biotechnology 停止了 PDS0101 治疗 HPV16 阳性头颈鳞状细胞癌的 3 期 VERSATILE-003 试验,并转向 PDS0301 治疗转移性结直肠癌。这一决定是基于有关资源分配和临床潜力的战略考虑而做出的。
规模、影响与风险
PDS0101 试验的停止代表着该公司研究重点的重大转变,对参加该试验的患者具有潜在影响。与这一支点相关的风险在于 PDS0301 在结直肠癌中未经证实的治疗潜力,特别是其剂量毒性特征和实现有意义的肿瘤微环境重塑的能力。
应对、状态与不确定性
该公司已停止 PDS0101 试验并转向 PDS0301,但没有提供有关该决定的具体原因的更多细节。该回应基于内部战略评估,PDS0301试验的结果仍不确定。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

PDS Biotechnology Halts PDS0101 Trial, Pivots to PDS0301 in Colorectal Cancer - The Clinical Trial Vanguard

News Brief
PDS Biotechnology has halted its Phase 3 VERSATILE-003 trial for PDS0101, a treatment for HPV16-positive head and neck squamous cell carcinoma, and shifted focus to PDS0301 for metastatic colorectal cancer. This decision, announced in an August 11 shareholder letter, reflects the company's strategic prioritization of resources toward a potentially more differentiated opportunity. The VERSATILE-002 Phase 2 trial had shown a 39.3-month median overall survival in combination with pembrolizumab, but stopping before Phase 3 means this data cannot mature into a label claim. The company now bets on PDS0301, an IL-12 immunocytokine designed to target tumors and reduce systemic toxicity. However, the success of PDS0301 depends on demonstrating clean tolerability at therapeutic doses, which remains uncertain. The pivot signals a shift in focus from HPV16-associated cancers to colorectal cancer, where immunotherapy penetration is still a challenge. The decision was disclosed simultaneously with the trial halt, leaving no ambiguity about the replacement strategy.
Original source text (English)
Moe Alsumidaie Chief Editor Moe Alsumidaie is Chief Editor of The Clinical Trial Vanguard. Moe holds decades of experience in the clinical trials industry. Moe also serves as Head of Research at CliniBiz and Chief Data Scientist at Annex Clinical Corporation. Adaptive Clinical Trial Design Adaptive Trial Design Addiction Psychiatry AI in Clinical Trials alcohol use disorder Alzheimer's Disease Article Cell and Gene Therapy Clinical Operations Clinical Research Clinical Trial Design Clinical Trial Operations Clinical Trial Ops Brief Clinical Trial Quality Clinical Trials CLINOPS WATCHDOG CNS Trials Conference Coverage Decentralized Clinical Trials Digital Health Technologies dual diagnosis eClinical Tech News eClinical Technology EMA Executive Interviews FDA Enforcement FDA Guidance FDA Regulation FDA Regulatory Strategy FDA Warning Letter GCP Compliance GLP-1 Receptor Agonists Ketamine Therapy Oncology Trials Opinion Parkinson's Disease Pediatric Oncology Platform Trials Psychedelic Clinical Trials PSYCH PULSE Real-World Evidence site activation Site Operations study startup Treatment-Resistant Depression Search for:Search Button Articles Conference Coverage Executive Interviews News CliniCast News PDS Biotechnology Halts PDS0101 Trial, Pivots to PDS0301 in Colorectal Cancer By Jon Napitupulu August 12, 2026 PDS Biotechnology built its public identity around PDS0101, and a reported 39.3-month median overall survival in HPV16-positive first-line recurrent and metastatic head and neck squamous cell carcinoma made that attachment understandable. Abandoning it anyway, as the company announced in an August 11 shareholder letter filed as an 8-K with the SEC, is the kind of move that demands explanation beyond routine pipeline pruning. The decision to halt internal investment in PDS0101, including the discontinuation of the Phase 3 VERSATILE-003 trial, signals that management concluded the capital required to run a confirmatory head and neck study could not be justified against what it sees as a more tractable opportunity elsewhere. That opportunity is PDS0301, a tumor-targeted IL-12 immunocytokine designed to remodel the tumor microenvironment, now repositioned as the company’s lead asset with metastatic colorectal cancer as the priority indication. The strategic logic is legible: IL-12 has long been recognized as a potent but toxicity-prone cytokine, and a tumor-targeted formulation attempts to confine activity to the lesion rather than flooding systemic circulation. Whether PDS0301 can deliver that profile at therapeutic doses in colorectal cancer is the clinical question the company is now betting its resources on. The pivot was disclosed simultaneously with the VERSATILE-003 halt, leaving no ambiguity about sequencing: this is a replacement, not a parallel track. The sacrifice of PDS0101 data is genuinely costly. The VERSATILE-002 Phase 2 result, a 39.3-month median overall survival in combination with pembrolizumab, was a credible signal in a setting where pembrolizumab is already an established standard of care. Stopping before Phase 3 readout means that number never matures into a label claim. For patients with HPV16-positive disease, that gap is real. For the company, the calculus was apparently that a fully resourced Phase 3 program in HNSCC, competing for attention against established pembrolizumab combination data, was less defensible than an earlier-stage bet in colorectal cancer where immunotherapy penetration remains a harder, but potentially more differentiated, problem. The single clinical marker to watch now is the dose-toxicity profile of PDS0301 in its colorectal studies. IL-12’s history of systemic inflammatory toxicity has ended promising programs before, and if PDS0301 cannot demonstrate clean tolerability at doses that produce meaningful tumor microenvironment remodeling, the logic of abandoning a 39.3-month survival asset collapses entirely. 解读特朗普关于美国的行政命令 疫苗接种 - dw.com

Making sense of Trump's executive order on US vaccinations - dw.com
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 10 日,美国总统唐纳德·特朗普签署了一项行政命令,将儿童 疫苗 的建议数量从 18 减少到 11,并将剩余的 疫苗 重新分类为基于风险和共同决策类别。此举符合世界卫生组织的建议,将乙型肝炎、轮状病毒、流感、呼吸道合胞病毒和水痘从基线计划中删除。美国儿科学会批评该命令缺乏“黄金标准科学”,而疾病预防控制中心此前表示,没有证据支持 MMR 疫苗 的分裂。公共卫生专家警告称,该政策可能会破坏 疫苗接种 共识,理由是最近美国和欧洲麻疹感染人数的上升与 疫苗接种 发病率下降和反 疫苗 言论有关。

美国;一般儿童人口和高危人群。

President Donald Trump signed an executive order on 2026-08-10 titled 'Delivering gold standard childhood vaccine recommendations for Americans,' which reduces the recommended number of childhood vaccines from 18 to 11 and reclassifies remaining vaccines into risk-based and shared-decision categories. The order is reported as not based on 'gold-standard science' by the American Academy of Pediatrics.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Making sense of Trump's executive order on US vaccinations - dw.com

News Brief
On August 10, 2026, U.S. President Donald Trump signed an executive order reducing the recommended number of childhood vaccines from 18 to 11 and reclassifying remaining vaccines into risk-based and shared-decision categories. The move, framed as aligning with World Health Organization recommendations, removes hepatitis B, rotavirus, influenza, RSV, and varicella from the baseline schedule. The American Academy of Pediatrics criticized the order as lacking 'gold-standard science,' while the CDC has previously stated there is no evidence supporting the splitting of the MMR vaccine. Public health experts warn the policy could destabilize vaccination consensus, citing recent rises in measles infections in the U.S. and Europe linked to reduced vaccination rates and anti-vaccine rhetoric.
Original source text (English)
You need to enable JavaScript to run this app. Skip to content Skip to main menu Skip to more DW sites Latest videos Latest audio Regions Africa Asia Europe Latin America Middle East North America Germany Topics Climate Equality Health Human Rights Migration Technology Categories Business Science Environment Culture Sports In focus German politicsUkraineHeat and drought Latest audioLatest videos Live TV Advertisement ScienceUnited States of America Making sense of Trump's executive order on US vaccinations Zulfikar Abbany 08/11/2026August 11, 2026 President Donald Trump and his Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. have long tried to reduce the number of vaccinations available to the US public and even farm animals. The latest move in context. https://p.dw.com/p/5Id3b Copy link The Trump Administration wants to separate the combined measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) vaccine into single shotsImage: Rebecca Blackwell/AP Photo/picture alliance Advertisement US President Donald Trump signed another executive order on Monday, August 10, 2026: "Delivering gold standard childhood vaccine recommendations for Americans." The order reduces the current recommendation of 18 vaccines for children down to 11, which puts it almost in line with the World Health Organization (WHO) recommendations. As DW reported in May 2025, there are vaccines against 30 infectious diseases. The World Health Organization recommends that every child get vaccinated against 12 of those: Tuberculosis Hepatitis B Polio Diphtheria Tetanus Pertussis (whooping cough) Haemophilus influenza type B Pneumococcal disease Rotavirus Measles Rubella Human papillomavirus This is the WHO making recommendations for all nations — both rich and poor. Many poor countries face challenges around access to vaccines at prices they can afford and distributing them safely to people living in remote areas. Protecting vaccines in Ghana's rising heat To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video 02:01 Rich countries, on the other hand, can afford and therefore usually offer more vaccinations than the WHO's base level of 12. But not all of them do. Germany, for example, recommends 14 vaccinations for children in their first two years after birth. Germany no longer recommends the BCG vaccine against tuberculosis (TB), which is on the WHO list. It does recommend vaccination against Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV), which is not on the WHO list. This just goes to show that there are variations around the world. It's not just the US. These variations often depend on which diseases are considered by local health experts to be an ongoing threat to any given community. Taking the example of TB in Germany: In 1998, the country's Standing Committee on Vaccination (STIKO) decided the risk of TB infections was low, and herd immunity was deemed high enough at 50-80%. Therefore the STIKO stopped recommending the vaccine. Advertisement What does Trump's new childhood vaccine recommendation say? Trump wants to create three distinct categories for vaccination. The first category includes immunizations recommended for all children: measles, mumps, rubella, diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis, polio, Haemophilus influenzae type b (meningitis), pneumococcal disease, human papillomavirus and varicella (chickenpox). That removes vaccination against hepatitis B, rotavirus, influenza and RSV from this baseline category that were on the recommended list until now. These illnesses however can still affect newborns. As we reported in 2022, cases of RSV have risen in the US and Europe. Babies who don't receive the vaccine would remain unprotected against the illness. By contrast, Germany recommends vaccination for RSV, rotavirus and hepatitis B in a child's first six months. Trump's executive order also recommends that the combined MMR vaccine for measles, mumps and rubella — which is a global standard — be split into individual jabs, spaced over time. This appears to be a reflection on long-held rhetoric from the Trump administration suggesting that the combined MMR vaccine causes autism. The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) says there is no published evidence to suggest there is any benefit in single jabs. In fact, its advice on the MMR vaccine states that "the CDC and the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) agree that MMR vaccine is not responsible for recent increases in the number of children with autism." But Trump and Robert F. Kennedy Jr. have long suggested vaccines are dangerous in general. Kennedy also thinks that the common painkiller Tylenol causes autism when taken during pregnancy. It is the only painkiller allowed during pregnancy in the US. And as we reported in September 2025, no high-quality scientific evidence substantiated their claim. Is the US experiencing a vaccine crisis? To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video 05:18 Trump's second category would include immunizations for high-risk groups or populations: respiratory syncytial virus monoclonal antibodies, hepatitis A, hepatitis B, meningococcal B, meningococcal ACWY, and dengue. The third would include vaccines to be given "based on shared clinical decision-making" — so, doctor and patient or parent of a patient would have to agree. These include: hepatitis A, hepatitis B, rotavirus, meningococcal disease, influenza and COVID-19. The effect of political — rather than scientific — rhetoric on vaccines While there's no direct recommendation to ban any vaccine, the second Trump administration has repeatedly attempted to destabilize public consensus over vaccination. That can cause confusion in the community and rising rates of infection. For example, in July 2026, DW reported how "amnesia" about measles and how dangerous it is had led to a rise in measles infections in the US. People had missed opportunities to get vaccinated because infection rates had been low, but then they rose again. This is not just the case in North America. Germany has also seen measles infections rise, with experts citing anti-vaccination rhetoric that emerged during the COVID-19 pandemic. Allowing bird flu to spread Kennedy however takes his hands-off rhetoric to farming communities, too. In March 2025, DW reported how the health secretary suggested bird flu should be allowed to spread through bird populations, even in places where it had already infected cattle. Kennedy said it would enable researchers to develop "super" chickens. But instead, more than 150 million birds got infected. Bird flu virus was detected in raw milk. And there were bird-to-human infections as well. On Monday, when Trump's executive order was released, the AAP's Andrew Racine said it was "not based on 'gold-standard science.' There is no new evidence to justify significant changes to childhood immunization guidance." Edited by: Carla Bleiker This article was updated on August 13, 2026, to correct an error in the original version. We had equated varicella virus with smallpox when it is in fact chickenpox. Time
Published 2026-08-12; event date 2026-08-10 (executive order signed). Not reported in the supplied evidence.
Location and population
United States; general child population and high-risk groups.
Event
President Donald Trump signed an executive order on 2026-08-10 titled 'Delivering gold standard childhood vaccine recommendations for Americans,' which reduces the recommended number of childhood vaccines from 18 to 11 and reclassifies remaining vaccines into risk-based and shared-decision categories. The order is reported as not based on 'gold-standard science' by the American Academy of Pediatrics.
Scale, impact and risk
You need to enable JavaScript to run this app.
Response, status and uncertainty
The American Academy of Pediatrics states there is no new evidence to justify the changes; the order is described as politically driven rather than scientifically grounded. The CDC previously stated no benefit to splitting the MMR vaccine. The order does not ban any vaccine but reassigns them to categories, potentially reducing baseline coverage for hepatitis B, rotavirus, influenza, RSV, and varicella. The policy may contribute to public confusion and rising infection rates, as seen with measles in the U.S.

Original source text (English)
click Local News Sports Opinion NKY Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTH Q&A: Local health experts weigh in on child vaccines, new Trump guidelines Esther Launstein Cincinnati Enquirer Updated Aug. 11, 2026, 3:06 p.m. ET This story was updated because an earlier version included an inaccuracy. The Trump Administration recommends that state health agencies reduce the number of childhood vaccines and separate the doses of commonly combined immunizations, according to an executive order released Aug. 10. The order changes the recommended vaccines for all children and advises that commonly combined vaccinations, including the measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) vaccine, be administered in three separate single-disease shots at separate medical appointments. But what do these recommendations mean for children in Ohio? The Enquirer spoke with Dr. Steve Feagins, the medical director at Hamilton County Public Health, and Dr. Chris Peltier, a board-certified pediatrician at Pediatric Associates of Mt. Carmel and previous president of the Ohio chapter of the American Academy of Pediatrics. This interview has been edited for brevity and clarity. What are the current recommendations for childhood vaccinations? Feagins: The recommendations have not changed, nor has the law. The requirements for the 10 diseases that children are vaccinated against prior to entering kindergarten is Ohio State law. And so we, your local health department, follow the guidelines and the requirements of the Ohio Department of Health, which typically takes its guidelines from the CDC. Peltier: There is no new research or evidence that supports the executive order that was signed yesterday. We know from decades of research and thousands of studies that vaccines are safe. Vaccines work. They do not cause autism, and really the schedule that the American Academy of Pediatrics has put out has been well-studied and is based on evidence. Recommended vaccine schedule from the American Academy of Pediatrics: At birth: Hepatitis B; respiratory syncytial virus. 2 months old: Diphtheria, tetanus, whooping cough (DTaP) combined vaccine; pneumococcal conjugate; Haemophilus influenzae tybe B; polio; and rotavirus. 1 year old: measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) combined; chicken pox / varicella; hepatitis A; COVID-19; and influenza. Teens: Tetanus, diphtheria and acellular pertussis (Tdap) combined vaccine; meningococcal meningitis; and human papillomavirus. Recommended changes from the Trump Administration: All children: measles, mumps, rubella, diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis, polio, Haemophilus influenzae type B, pneumococcal disease, human papillomavirus and varicella. High-risk populations: respiratory syncytial virus monoclonal antibodies, hepatitis A, hepatitis B, meningococcal B, meningococcal ACWY, and dengue. What is the reasoning behind the Trump Administration's recommended changes to the vaccine schedule? Peltier: The rationale that we have heard, both from the Secretary (Robert F.) Kennedy (Jr.) and Health and Human Services as well as the president, is that they are trying to align our vaccine schedule closer to that of Denmark. Denmark does fewer vaccinations that we do in the United States. The problem is we don't live in Denmark. It's a different country, different population and obviously a much different size. They have a much different health system than we do. Denmark has opted to invest more money in reactive health care. So, if a child does get a vaccine preventable illness, their health system is much more equipped to take care of those kids. Whereas that's not the case in this country. So, our goal is to prevent illness because oftentimes we know that our health care systems are undermanned and Medicaid is being slashed. Funding is being cut, which really decreases the care that kids get once they get sick. You can't compare healthcare in Denmark to healthcare in the United States. It just doesn't make sense. How are vaccine schedules developed? Feagins: The various experts on adult vaccinations as well as childhood diseases come together in a prescribed fashion to develop the guidelines. And we've followed those from the Academy for decades, and they've very much been in line with the CDC guidelines historically. Peltier: Vaccinating kids against vaccine preventable illnesses obviously prevents those illnesses. One recommended change is giving measles, mumps and rubella as separate vaccines over several appointments. What would be the impact? Peltier: There was talk in the press conference yesterday about MMR causing fatal reactions. That is just not true. The evidence does not support that. There has never been a death due to the MMR vaccine. There is no science to back up splitting them up. We know the science supports giving a combined MMR vaccine at age 12 months and at age 4 years. It's safe. There really isn't any good science to support that recommendation. It really does a couple of things. It's only going to end up delaying vaccination rates, delaying getting kids completely vaccinated, and it's going to cause more disease because now families have to come to a physician's office three different times. Those have to be would have to be separated by at least a month because they're live virus vaccines, and you have to wait a month minimum in between administering those. The other thing is, there currently is no manufacturer that makes single measles, mumps and rubella vaccines. That would take time, an investment in capital, and I'm not sure there's really any incentive for a vaccine manufacturer to invest that kind of money to develop three new vaccines that we don't need. The Trump Administration also cited links between vaccines and autism as reasons for the changes. What does science show? Peltier: The science shows us that there is no link. There is study after study involving thousands of children that show no link between vaccines and autism. It just doesn't exist. Feagins: There have been large national and international studies comparing large numbers of children who received vaccinations and those who did not. And the incidence of autism remains the same. And so, we conclude from that that there does not appear to be a cause-and-effect relationship to vaccinations and subsequent development of autism. What advice would you give to parents about their vaccination options going into the schoolyear? Feagins: I don't know any parents who don't want the best for their children. I don't know any physicians or clinicians who don't want the best for their patients, and so the advice is have the conversation, talk with your trusted clinician and decide on what's the best course for you. As we enter the school year, we just wanted to point out that it's a good thing to get your vaccines get lined up. These vaccines are for a reason, so that you cannot just protect your child but all children, including those who may not be vaccinated. Peltier: It's completely natural to have questions when you're a parent. You want to do what's best and safe for your kids. So my best advice would be talk to your pediatrician, ask them questions about vaccines so that we can share with you why we do what we do, why we follow the American Academy of Pediatric schedule. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

该市 18,377 名女孩接受了 HPV 疫苗 - 印度时报

18,377 girls administered HPV vaccine in city - The Times of India
新闻简报印度坎普尔发起了一项为 14 至 15 岁少女免费注射 HPV 疫苗 的运动,以预防 宫颈癌。截至2026年8月9日,已有18,377名女孩接种了疫苗,区县长正在检查疫苗接种工作,并敦促家长确保女儿接种疫苗。地区免疫官员确认了 疫苗 在预防 宫颈癌 方面的安全性和有效性。目标为 59,450 疫苗接种,但仍有 41,073 名女孩面临风险。政府正在周日组织特别的 疫苗接种 营地,并开展公众宣传活动,以惠及所有符合条件的女孩。该活动旨在通过广泛的 疫苗接种 和教育来降低目标人群中的 宫颈癌 风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
HPV 疫苗 疫苗接种活动于坎普尔启动,区长于周日检查了 疫苗接种 工作,疫苗接种 会议每周三和周六持续进行。发布日期为 2026-08-09。
地点与对象
该活动在印度坎普尔举行,专门针对该地区 14 至 15 岁的少女。
事件
据《印度时报》报道,坎普尔符合条件的少女正在免费注射 HPV 疫苗,以预防 宫颈癌。区县长检查了疫苗接种工作并强调了疫苗接种的重要性。
规模、影响与风险
迄今为止,已有 18,377 名青春期女孩接受了 HPV 疫苗 注射。该地区 41,073 名尚未接种疫苗的女孩仍然面临 宫颈癌 的风险。HPV 疫苗 被描述为降低 宫颈癌 风险的安全有效的方法。
应对、状态与不确定性
政府免费提供 HPV 疫苗,官员们正在开展公众宣传活动,以确保所有符合条件的女孩都接种疫苗。特别的 疫苗接种 营地将于周日组织,卫生部门正在确保不遗漏任何符合条件的女孩。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

18,377 girls administered HPV vaccine in city - The Times of India

News Brief
In Kanpur, India, a campaign to administer the HPV vaccine free of cost to adolescent girls aged 14 to 15 years has been launched to prevent cervical cancer. As of August 9, 2026, 18,377 girls have been vaccinated, with the district magistrate inspecting the vaccination work and urging parents to ensure their daughters receive the vaccine. The district immunization officer confirmed the vaccine's safety and effectiveness in preventing cervical cancer. A target of 59,450 vaccinations has been set, leaving 41,073 girls still at risk. The government is organizing special vaccination camps on Sundays and conducting public awareness campaigns to reach all eligible girls. The campaign aims to reduce the risk of cervical cancer among the target population through widespread vaccination and education.

District magistrate Jitendra Pratap Singh, who reached the Urban Primary Health Centre in Rawatpur on Sunday to inspect the vaccination work and appealed to parents to get their eligible daughters vaccinated. So far, 18,377 adolescent girls in the district have been administered the HPV vaccine.The DM said that infection with the Human Papilloma Virus is a major cause of cervical cancer. The HPV vaccine is a safe and effective means of reducing the risk of this cancer in the future. Along with HPV vaccination for the prevention of cervical cancer, timely screening at the prescribed age is also important. This vaccine, which is expensive in private hospitals, is being made available completely free of cost to eligible adolescent girls by the government.He appealed to parents not to fall for any confusion, hesitation or rumours and to take their daughters to the vaccination centre. . He directed Health Department officials to run a wide public awareness campaign regarding HPV vaccination and ensure that no eligible adolescent girl is left out of vaccination.During the inspection, the DM took information about the presence of doctors and health workers, vaccination arrangements, and the health services being provided to patients. by Taboolaby Taboola Sponsored LinksSponsored Links Promoted LinksPromoted Links You May Like Full Mouth Dental Implants Cost Without Insurance (Take A Look)EliteSearches | Full Mouth Implants Learn More Undo He said that people who are unable to reach the health centre, can visit the nearest Ayushman Arogya Mandir or Urban Primary Health Centre on Sunday to avail health check-ups, medical consultation and available vaccination services. He directed that patients coming on Sunday should not face any inconvenience and that information about the available services should be conveyed to the general public.Under the campaign, the HPV vaccine is being administered during regular vaccination sessions every Wednesday and Saturday. Special vaccination camps are also being organised on Sunday. ASHA and Anganwadi workers have prepared a due list to identify eligible adolescent girls. On this basis, parents are being informed about vaccination through door-to-door contact.District immunization officer Dr SP Yadav said that the HPV vaccine is a safe and effective measure to prevent cervical cancer. BoxRisk of cervical cancer remains for 41,073 adolescent girls in KanpurA target has been set to administer the HPV vaccine to 59,450 adolescent girls in the 14-15 years age group in the district. Of these, 18,377 adolescent girls have been vaccinated so far. In this way, 41,073 adolescent girls are still outside the protection cover provided by the HPV vaccine and the risk of cervical cancer remains among them. View five news elements
Time
The HPV vaccine administration campaign started in Kanpur, with the district magistrate inspecting the vaccination work on Sunday, and the vaccination sessions are ongoing every Wednesday and Saturday. The publication date is 2026-08-09.
Location and population
The event took place in Kanpur, India, specifically targeting adolescent girls aged 14 to 15 years in the district.
Event
The HPV vaccine is being administered free of cost to eligible adolescent girls in Kanpur to prevent cervical cancer, as reported by The Times of India. The district magistrate inspected the vaccination work and emphasized the importance of vaccination.
Scale, impact and risk
So far, 18,377 adolescent girls have been administered the HPV vaccine. The risk of cervical cancer remains for 41,073 girls in the district who have not yet been vaccinated. The HPV vaccine is described as a safe and effective means of reducing the risk of cervical cancer.
Response, status and uncertainty
The government is providing the HPV vaccine free of cost, and officials are conducting public awareness campaigns to ensure all eligible girls are vaccinated. Special vaccination camps are being organized on Sundays, and the Health Department is ensuring no eligible girl is left out.
